### Welcome To The Course! {#welcome-to-the-course}

```
Hello and welcome to the course.
My name is Khan and I'm super excited to have you here.
I'm going to be your instructor in this course, and I'm going to take you along this journey of learning
C++ and learning the basics of the Unreal Engine.
I'm going to teach you everything that you need to know, so that you can start making awesome games
on your own.
Let's take a look at the layout of the course.
The course is made out of five different sections.
First is the introduction, the one that you're currently in, and after that we have four amazing game
projects in the each section that follows.
In this first section, we will start by learning how to install Unreal Engine and how to set it up.
After that, we're going to create a project and start getting our feet wet.
Immediately.
We're going to learn necessary basics like navigating the interface, how to place down and move actors.
We're also going to learn how to use C++ in the context of Unreal Engine, so there's a lot of things
to do to learn.
Let's get started.
```

## Battle Blaster {#battle-blaster}

### Moving & Placing Actors {#moving-placing-actors}

```
Hello and welcome.
In this lecture, you're going to learn how to place down shape actors into the level and modify their
transforms.
Let's get started.
Okay, so first of all, let's learn how to place down a shape actor into the level.
We need to go into this menu right here which is called quickly add to the project.
If you left click on that and go into the shape section.
We have options here to place down into the level.
We have a cube, a sphere, a cylinder, a cone and a plane.
In this case I'm going to place down a cube.
We need to left click on it, hold and then drag it into the viewport.
And you can see that we get this nice little preview of the cube.
We can just leave it here into the game.
That's going to create a new cube actor.
As you can also see from the outliner, it's called cube.
It's going to be automatically selected.
And you can see that we get these three arrows when we have it selected.
If you don't see these, you need to go into the move tool, which you can do by clicking on this button
right here.
Or you can also use the shortcut which is the W key.
Using these arrows, we can change the location of this actor inside of the game world.
As you can see, these arrows represent the three different dimensions we have in the game X, which
is the red arrow, Y which is the green arrow, and Z which is the blue arrow.
You can think of Z as going up and down, and you can think of X and y as moving inside of the ground
plane.
You can actually go up and look at this from top, and you can see that x and y are essentially going
left and right and forwards and backwards on the ground, and Z is going to be going up and down just
like that.
It's a good way to think about these dimensions.
We can see the textual or number representation of the location of this actor inside of the details
panel.
So while you have the cube selected, you can take a look at the details.
There's a section called the transform.
And in the transform we have the location which is going to show us the X, the Y, and the z positions
of this actor.
You can see that on the x we're at -840.
On the Y we're at -1000.
And on the Z we're at positive 160.
And as I play with these arrows you can see that those numbers are changing.
We can also change them in the details panel directly as well.
We don't need to use the arrows all the time.
So for example I could set the x to be -500 and it's going to instantly teleport to that location.
But what do these numbers mean.
What is -500.
What is -1000.
In unreal one unit is equal to one centimeter of distance.
In that case, -500 would be five meters in the negative direction on the x axis.
Likewise -1000 would be ten meters in the negative direction on the y axis, so keep that in mind.
One unit in unreal equals to one centimetre.
And the negative and positive numbers are going to represent the direction.
So for example on the x if we pull the cube in this direction you can see that that's going to be positive.
If we go in the opposite direction that's going to be negative on the Y.
If we go in that direction it will be negative, this direction will be positive and Z going up is going
to be positive and going down is going to be negative.
These just help us represent the position of an actor in the 3D game world.
And this is the same for all actors.
You can click on any one of these, and you can see their specific location, and you can move them
as you wish.
If you want to take back something that you did like this one, you can hit the control Z shortcut to
undo what you did.
Or you can also go into the edit menu right here and click it manually, which is going to be this one.
Okay.
So that is the location of an actor.
But we're not limited to just changing the location of it.
We can also change the rotation and the scale to change the rotation.
We're going to go into the rotation tool, which you can also go into by using the E shortcut.
That's going to give you a different set of gizmos.
And from here you can change the rotation of this in three dimensions just like that.
You can see that values are being changed in the transform as well.
Inside of the details.
You can also play with these here.
Just like that you can left click and scroll or drag the mouse to change it.
If you want to reset one of these properties like rotation or the location, you can just click on the
arrow that appears next to it, and that's going to take back all of the rotation changes that you did.
Next up we have the scale, which you can change by going into the scale tool from here in the toolbar.
```

## Shooter Sam {#shooter-sam}

### Compilers and Editors {#compilers-and-editors}

```
Hello and welcome.
In this lecture, we're going to learn some very important concepts that we need to understand before
we can start writing C plus plus code.
First of all we're going to learn what source code is.
Source code is human readable code in this case C plus plus.
But it's a general term used for all programming languages.
It's stored in a text file with a special file extension for C plus.
Plus that is CP or dot h.
So whenever you see a file with dot cp or dot h file extension, you can be sure that it contains C
plus plus source code.
Now this part is important human readable because the CPU or the processor you have inside of your computer
doesn't understand source code.
It actually has no idea what C plus plus is.
The CPU only understands machine code which is written in ones and zeros in binary.
But because it would be almost impossible to write code in machine code, humans have invented programming
languages like C plus plus so that we can write human readable source code, and then we can turn that
into machine code for the CPU to understand.
So that is what source code is.
Here's an example of it.
You can see that this is a file called my CPP.
That's how you know it contains C plus plus source code.
And in here we have some 40 lines of code.
You don't need to understand any of this just yet.
I just want you to see what it looks like.
Next up we're going to talk about the compiler, which comes into play when we want to take our source
code and we want to turn it into machine code for the CPU to understand.
Compiler is simply a program that's going to take in that source code that you wrote, and it's going
to convert it into machine code ones and zeros.
It's also going to create an executable file that we can actually run on windows.
This would be a exe file.
In this case we're not going to have to create that actually because we're going to directly run our
machine code inside of unreal.
The compiler we're going to use on PC is going to be the Visual Studio compiler.
And on Mac we're going to use the Xcode compiler.
Next up, let's talk about the source code editor.
This is a piece of software that's going to help us edit our source code.
It's essentially a very fancy text editor.
It's going to provide us with features like code completion, syntax highlighting, and it's going to
show errors that we might have inside of our code.
And it's going to have a lot more features than that actually.
If you don't know what syntax means, it's simply the rules or the way we type in the C plus plus programming
language into a text file.
The source code editor is going to make it easier and more efficient to write C plus plus code, and
it's going to make it more fun.
Here is an example of a source code editor Visual Studio.
You can see that on the right side we have the files we have inside of this project.
The selected file is displayed in the middle here where we can edit it and make changes to it.
You can see that some of these words are displayed in a different color.
That is the syntax highlighting.
It's making it easier for us to read the code.
For example, these green lines here that start with two forward slashes.
These are called comments.
And you can see that all of these are green and at a glance you can easily differentiate all of them
from the other lines of code.
So like I said, it's making it much easier for us to read what we have in the file.
You also might have noticed that a second ago we said the compiler was Visual Studio, but now we're
saying the source code editor is also Visual Studio.
That is correct.
Visual studio has both the compiler and the source code editor capabilities inside of it, very similar
to what we're going to have on Mac.
Xcode is going to be the compiler and the source code editor at the same time, just like Visual Studio.
Visual Studio and Xcode are also called Ides, which stands for Integrated Development Environment,
which is just a fancy word for a text editor that has extra capabilities for development purposes.
So let's do a final recap of this process.
In the first step, we're going to type out our cplusplus source code in cpp and files.
It's going to look something like this.
And like I said this is going to be the human readable text based instructions that we're going to write.
We're going to give this over to the compiler, and the compiler is going to turn this into machine
code ones and zeros.
As you can see, we're not going to be able to understand what any of this is.
But that's not important.
The important part is that we can actually execute this and run this because the CPU understands this
code, because we're working with the Unreal Engine, we're not going to run the machine code ourselves.
We're going to give it to the editor.
If we are testing the game inside of the editor, or we're going to give it to the Unreal Engine when
we package the game up so that we can run it as a standalone game when making a game in unreal.
90% of the time you're going to test it in the editor, and then finally, once it's ready, you can
package it up as a standalone game if you want to share it or send it to your friends.
So that is the journey of our source code, from being written to being compiled into machine code and
then being executed within the editor or within a standalone game.
Hopefully this whole process is much more clear now.
Now that we understand all of this, we can go ahead and install the compiler and the source code editor
that we're going to use.
```

### C++ vs Blueprint {#c-vs-blueprint}

```
Hello and welcome.
In this lecture we're going to talk about C++ versus blueprint.
At this point in the course we understand what C++ is.
Let's learn a little bit more about it.
Let's also learn what blueprints are, why we need them and when to use which.
So first of all, when we're making games with Unreal Engine, we have two choices for writing code
C++ or blueprint.
C++ is a general purpose programming language.
We need to create source code files to write C++ that end with that cpp or that h, and we need to use
an editor like Visual Studio.
We also need to compile our source code into machine code before we can run it inside of the editor.
Blueprint, on the other hand, is the visual scripting system that is built into the Unreal Engine
editor.
It looks like this.
As you can see, it's not text based.
Instead of that, you are creating these nodes that have functionality and you're connecting them using
these arrows.
You don't need to use an external editor like Visual Studio.
You can program blueprint directly in the editor using the event Graph section of each blueprint.
So those are the two options we have when it comes to writing code.
And C++ and blueprint actually complement each other, and they're meant to be used together.
So you can think of them as friends.
They both have strengths and weaknesses, and it really comes down to understanding both of them and
knowing when to use which.
So let's quickly talk about some strengths of both of them.
Starting with blueprints.
Blueprint is much easier to learn, especially at the beginning compared to C++, because it's completely
visual.
You don't need to learn how to type any code, you can just use the prebuilt nodes to create your logic.
It's programmed inside of unreal, so you don't even have to learn another editor like Visual Studio.
You can just do everything within unreal, which I think is very cool.
It's very quick to make changes.
So if you're trying to prototype something very quickly, it's very good for that.
But at the same time, that kind of becomes a weakness because blueprint kind of gets messy as you're
trying to build bigger and bigger games, it becomes harder to manage all of the visual nodes that you're
creating.
And finally, it's very good for writing gameplay code, but it's not really used for much else besides
writing gameplay code.
Now with C++.
C++ gives us full control over all areas of unreal and beyond.
We can access more functionality of the Unreal Engine libraries from C++, but in blueprint we are kind
of limited to doing gameplay programming.
I said and beyond here because we can use other people's libraries in C++, not just on reals libraries.
And that is because C++ is a general purpose language.
We can take any C++ library and use it, which is very cool.
C++ is much faster than anything that's written in blueprint.
So when performance is a question, C++ is always going to win.
It's much more concise compared to blueprints.
You can express more logic with less C++ code compared to blueprint, and it's much better for writing
bigger games.
It's much easier to organize bigger projects and more code.
Also, if you're writing non-gameplay code, you're almost always going to use C++.
So those are some strengths of both parties here.
But like I said, at the end of the day, they're meant to be used together.
So to give an overview of how everything fits together, we have the Unreal Engine libraries written
in C++.
We're going to write our own C++ code.
We're going to be able to access the Unreal Engine libraries from our own C++ code.
We're also going to write some blueprints.
And those blueprints are going to be able to access the Unreal Engine libraries and also our C++ code.
This connection here is important though, because the blueprint we write can access our C++ code,
but the C++ code we write can't access the blueprints.
That means we can use blueprints to extend the C++ code we wrote, but not vice versa.
In this course, we're mainly going to be writing C++ with just a little bit of blueprints.
And the good thing is, once you understand C++ very well, blueprints are very, very easy to learn.
It's going to be more difficult to get started with C++, but just put in the work.
Trust me.
And then blueprint is going to be extremely simple for you to understand.
So that's how blueprints work.
That's what they are and their relationship to C++.
Hopefully all of that is clear now.
```

### Geometry Brushes {#geometry-brushes}

```
Hello and welcome.
In this lecture, we're going to learn how to use geometry brushes so that we can create complex shapes.
Let's go ahead and get started okay.
So in the last lecture we created this simple level so that we can test the game.

in order to test the game better.

shape actors as well.
However, there's also a geometry menu here that contains tools that are a little bit more powerful.
So let's learn how to use these.
Starting with the box, I'm going to left click and hold and drag this into the level that is going
to create a box shape inside of the game.
As you can see at first look, it might be similar to the cube shape, but this one has different types
of tools that we can take advantage of.
So with these the brushes, you never want to change the scale from the transform.
Instead of that you're going to scroll down into brush settings.
Here we have x, y, z.
And these are going to control the scale or the size of the brush.
I'm going to make this wider on the x just like that.
Maybe set it to be 1000.
I'm going to make it thinner on the Y.
Set it to be something like 60.
And then on the Z maybe we'll make it taller.
So let's say something like 400.
I'm going to pull it up as well so that it's sitting on the floor.
So again, up until this point it might just look like a regular old shape actor.
But the fun really begins when we start to use the brush type here inside of the details panel.
By default, they're set to be additive, but there's an option to be subtractive as well.
So the best way to explain this is to create an example.
We're going to bring in another box brush.
We're going to set this to be subtractive.
That's going to make it disappear as you can tell.
And then we're going to place this inside of the other one.
So this is what subtractive means subtractive one.
The spectral brush is going to take out a chunk from the additive one.
So normally by using cube shapes, you would have to create multiple shapes to create something like
this.
And the real power of this comes into play when you start to move these.
You can tell this can update itself on the fly.
So this would almost be impossible to create with regular shapes.
Maybe this can be like a window up there.
We can combine the additive and subtractive ones to really create interesting looking shapes.
So next up I'm going to go into the menu again and bring in a cylinder.
By default it's only going to have like eight sides which kind of looks like an octagon.
We're going to go into the settings here and set the size of this to be something like 50 or 70.
That's going to start to look more like a cylinder.
Then I'm going to go into the rotation mode by hitting the E key and then rotate this 90 degrees on
the X.
And then I'm going to place this into the wall and move it down a little bit, just like that.
And then I'm going to go into the settings again and set the brush type to be subtractive, which is
going to take out like a circular chunk out of the wall.
And maybe this can be the door.
We can play the game and see what it looks like.
As you can see, it looks quite cool.
And don't worry about this yellow message.
It just happens.
When we're using virtual shadow maps.
We can go into the project settings and we can type in shadow map.
And then we can change the shadow map option to be just shadow maps.
And that warning is going to go away.

I used a box and a cylinder.
Maybe you can try out the different ones.
And then I also want you to use at least one subtractive brush so that you can take out a chunk and
```

### Live Coding {#live-coding}

```
Hello and welcome.
In this lecture we're going to learn how to use live coding.
Let's go ahead and get started.
Okay.
So live coding lets us compile our project without exiting the editor and without using Visual Studio.
We can use it by clicking on this button right here in the bottom panel.
Just make sure that you have it enabled.
Click on this three dot menu next to the button and make sure that Enable Live Coding is checked.
And after that we can click on this button to start it.
But first of all, let's switch over to Visual Studio and make a code change so that we have something
to compile.
I'm simply going to change these numbers to text by saying first, second, and third.
Then we can save this file.
Get back into the editor and click on Live Coding.
This window is going to pop up, and if it's successful, it's going to say live coding succeeded in
the editor.
And also in this window it's also going to say successfully linked patch.
Once that's done, you can minimize this or exit out of it.
And we can play the game and you will see that inside of the output log.
The changes we made are in effect.
So it's that simple.
This is all you need to do to make a change to your source code.
Use live coding to compile it and then you're good to go.
That being said, we have some rules of thumb that go with using live coding because in some cases you
might run into issues.
So first of all, live coding is best used when you're making changes to the cplusplus files.
If you're making changes to the header file, it's a good idea to compile from Visual Studio.
Does that mean live coding wouldn't work if you make header file changes?
No, it would still work, but if you're making too many of them, at some point things might start
behaving in a weird way and you might not really understand what's going on.
So as a rule of thumb, it's a good idea to compile from Visual Studio using rebuild if you're making
too many header file changes.
So that's the first rule.
The second one is going to be compiling from Visual Studio at the start or at the end of your work session.
So let's say that I was developing something, and I'm using live coding a bunch of times.
At the end of my work session, I should recompile from Visual Studio, or at the start of my next work
session, I should definitely recompile from Visual Studio.
So those are some rules of thumb for you to memorize.
Like I said, if you're making a header file change, compile from Visual Studio and at the start or
at the end of your work session, definitely compile from Visual Studio.
And after that, you can just go ahead and use live coding as many times as you want.
Lastly, let me demonstrate what it looks like to make a mistake here.
So let's say that we forgot to put these parentheses and the semicolon.
I'm just going to get rid of them.
Save the file and try to run live coding.
So instead of the usual green message we're getting this red build failed.
That means something went wrong.
And inside of this log message, unreal is going to tell us what went wrong.
So if we scroll to the right side, you can see that it's telling us inside of moving platform dot CP
on line 23, the log is missing the terminating parenthesis.
So it's giving us an idea of what went wrong.
After taking a look at this, we can go back into Visual Studio.
We can go to line 23 as described in the error message.
And we can see that indeed we are missing those parentheses.
Visual studio is also being helpful.
And it's displaying this line with this red squiggly line and giving us a message about the problem
here.
Now we can go ahead and put the parentheses and the semicolon back there.
Save this and run live coding again.
And this time it's going to work.
So whenever you get an error you can take a look at the log message here that unreal gave you.
To understand try to understand what went wrong.
Take a look at Visual Studio.
Make sure that all of the syntax is the same as mine, and if all else fails, you can simply copy and
paste all of this log and share it as a question.
And we're going to take a look at it and help you out.
So that is how to address a compilation error in live coding.
And finally, as a challenge for you, you can go ahead and make the same changes as me, run live coding
and test this out.
So that is going to be it for this lecture.
Now that we can use live coding, we can compile our project much faster, which is going to make it
easier for us to develop the game.
Let's continue in the next lecture.
```

### Variables and Data Types {#variables-and-data-types}

```
Hello and welcome.
In this lecture we're going to learn about variables and data types.
Let's get started.
So what is a variable?
A variable is a container that can store a specific type of data.
You can think of it as a box with a name on it that is going to store a certain type of data that you're
going to provide.
For example, here we have power.
That is the name, and this stores a number which is 102.
Here we have a few more examples.
First of all we have a variable called my name.
And this is storing a string of text that says con which is my name.
Next up we have is alive.
That is the name of the variable.
And then we have the value which is true.
True is a Boolean type.
A boolean type can only be true or false.
In this case it is true.
And finally we have distance which is storing a floating point number, which is 7.34.
It is called a floating point number because it has a decimal point.
You can see that all of these variables have a unique name, and they all have a value.
And you can see that the type of value they have is different.
This one's a string.
This is a Boolean type.
And this is a floating point number.
The type of data a variable can store is determined by its data type, and every variable has a data
type, and it cannot store any other type of data.
So for example, an integer type of variable cannot store a floating point number and vice versa.
The basic data types we have in C plus plus are int for integers, float and double for floating point
numbers, char or kar for characters, and boolean for Boolean types.
True or false.
We also have an f string type.
This isn't a basic C plus plus data type.
This is something specific to unreal, but for all intents and purposes, you can think of it as a data
type right now that is going to store a string of text.
Let's take a look at the types of data these can store.
First of all we have int and this is going to store whole numbers.
Numbers like 1234 negative ten -200 numbers without a decimal point.
Then we have float and double.
And these are going to store floating point numbers.
3.14 -99.2.
Numbers like this these essentially do the same thing.
The only difference is a double can store larger numbers with a greater degree of accuracy.
We're mostly going to be using floats.
And in the case that we need to store larger numbers, we're going to use a double at the end of a decimal
point number.
You can place down an F to specify that this is a float, not a double.
Next up we have char which is going to store a single character.
And you can specify a single character with single quotes just like this, and then you're going to
place the character in between those.
We have Boolean or bool that can store a true or false value, and then we have f string, like I said,
which is something specific to unreal.
And this is going to store a string of characters just like this one.
And we know that we can specify a string with double quotes.
So pay attention to this.
A string has double quotes and a character has single quotes.
If you use double quotes with a character that turns it into a string with a single character inside
of it.
Okay, now that we have a basic understanding of variables and data types, let's go over to Visual
Studio to see these working in action.
All right.
So now let's learn how to declare and assign a value to a variable inside of our code.
We're going to continue to work in the Beginplay function.
And down here we're going to declare our first variable.
Let's go with an integer.
First of all we're going to type the name of the data type, which is going to be int for integer.
Then we're going to put a space and then we're going to give this variable a name.
In this case I'm going to give it the name of my integer my int.
And then we're going to put a semicolon to end the declaration of this variable.
You can see that we're continuing with the pattern of placing down a semicolon at the end of each statement.
It is something that you need to do in Cplusplus after you give the CPU a certain instruction.
In this case, we're telling the CPU to create a variable called my int up here we were making a call
to Ulog, and at the end of each of those statements we're putting a semicolon.
I'm saying statement instead of line because we can have multiple statements on a single line.
For example, I could take this declaration, cut it and place it after this.
And I can have two statements on a single line.
And this is completely fine in terms of the compiler, but it's just difficult to read.
That's why we usually put each statement on its own line to make it easier to read.
So this is how to declare a variable.
But you can see that this variable doesn't have any value to give it a value.
We need to use the assignment operator, which is the equal sign that you might be familiar with from
math.
In the case of programming, this is the assignment operator.
And it's going to assign a value to the left hand side.
So let's give it 100 as the value.
So like I said the assignment operator is going to assign a value.
It's going to take a look at the right hand side of it.
And it's going to take that value.
And it's going to assign it into the variable that's on the left hand side of it.
And this is called the initial value of a variable.
Because we're assigning it on the same line that we're declaring it.
After we make the declaration we can continue to assign different values to a variable.
So for example, I can say my integer.
Again use the assignment operator and this time assign 50.
So on this line we assigned it 100 as the initial value.
And on the next line we're assigning 50 to it.
So for all we know after this line here my INT is now 50.
And this pattern of creating a variable and assigning it is going to be the same for each type of variable
that we learned, whether it's a float double character, bool, all of them work the same way.
So now for your challenge, I want you to go ahead and declare a boolean variable using the bool keyword
and assign an initial value to it.
As a tip, you can only assign true or false to a boolean variable.
Pause the video.
Give this challenge a go and come back when you're done.

Then we're going to put a space.
And then we're going to give the name.
In this case I'm just going to call it my boolean.
And you can also probably tell the naming scheme of capitalizing the.
Each first letter of each word is continuing with the names of the variables.
So let's actually put a semicolon here.
And I'm going to talk briefly about the naming schemes we use.
But actually before that let's assign the initial value as well.
Let's make this true.
So this was the challenge declaring a boolean variable and giving it an initial value.
We set it to be true here and can also be false.
So those are the two different values a boolean variable have.
It cannot have anything else when it comes to naming schemes.
This is called the Pascal case capitalizing the first letter of each word.
We also have a convention called the camel case, and in that one the first word is capitalized, and
all the words after that has its first letter capitalized.
So if I had another word here, it would be capitalized as well.
This is camel case.
And then we also have snake case.
In that case no word is capitalized.
But we have underscores in between each word.
These are the most popular ones.
There's of course other types of naming conventions.
At the end of the day this purely comes down to preference, and it doesn't really have any actual performance
difference.
The important point is to pick one of these, pick one that you like and stick to it, because the worst
thing you can do is using different naming schemes in a single project.
In this course, we're mostly going to use the Pascal case.
So this is our boolean variable.
```

### Logging Variables {#logging-variables}

```
Hello and welcome.
In this lecture, we're going to learn how to include our variables inside of our log messages.
Let's go ahead and get started okay.
So at this point we know how to print out a plain text log message like the ones that we did up here.
Now we're going to learn how to include our variables like the ones that we created down here inside
of these messages.
This is going to be extremely important because it's going to let us see the values of our variables
inside of the editor, so that we have a better understanding of what the code is doing.
So let's go ahead and delete all of this.
And we're going to start off by creating a new integer variable.
The data type is int.
The name for this is going to be number of apples.
I'm going to set this to be ten because I have ten apples in my kitchen.
This is just for the sake of an example.
And then we're going to print this variable inside of our um log function call.
So let me re-explain how these function calls work.
So first of all you log is actually a macro instead of a function.
But they look exactly the same.
So at this point in the course we're not going to get into the details of that.
We're just going to continue calling it a function.
In order to make a function call.
We're going to type in the name of the function in this case log.
Then we're going to provide a set of parentheses.
And at the end of the line a semicolon to indicate the end of this statement.
Inside of the parentheses we're going to provide the arguments of the function.
The arguments are the values a function needs in order to operate properly.
Some functions don't have any arguments, so with them you can just leave the parentheses empty.
But some do require arguments.
In the case of log, the first argument is the log category.
We're going to provide log temp for this.
Temp stands for temporary.
We are always going to use the same category.
So you can just memorize this and keep using it.
Before the next argument we need to put down a comma.
And the next argument is going to be the level of importance.
We learned how to use display in the past.
We have a couple other options like warning, which I believe is going to display the log message in
yellow.
And there's also error which is going to display it in red.
In this case we're just going to continue using display for a regular log message.
After that we're going to place down another comma.
And then we're going to provide the text.
You log wants us to create a text using the text function.
This is also a macro actually.
But we're just going to continue calling it a function because it looks exactly the same.
Like I said, we're going to type in text in all capital letters and then put a set of parentheses inside
of them.
We're going to place down two double quotes.
And in that we know we can create a string that is going to be printed to the output log.
So so far everything is the same.
And now we're going to learn how to include a variable like this one right here inside of this text
message.
So first of all I'm going to type here number of apples colon.
And then I want to include the number of apples I have which is ten.
And to do that I'm going to place down a format specifier.
I can do that by placing down a percentage sign.
And then D the percentage sign is a special character.
That means I want to place down a variable inside of this string, and D means that this variable is
going to be an integer.
Then after the text here, I'm going to place down a comma and I'm going to provide number of apples.
I'm going to save it as well.
So once again, let me explain how this is going to work.
We have our text message right here.
It's saying number of apples colon percentage D which is the format specifier.
The percentage D is going to be replaced by number of apples.
So in the end it's going to go away and number of apples will be replaced.
So in this case number of apples is ten.
So this is going to be ten in the end.
So let's go ahead and make sure we save this file.
Go into unreal.
Compile it with Livecoding.
Play the game.
And you can see that we are printing number of apples colon ten.
The format specifier is replaced with the value of number of apples.
You can also see that the category is log temp and the level of importance is display, just like we
specified.
We can also print multiple of multiples of these inside of the same message.
We don't need to use a single one.
So for example I could place down another percentage D here.
And if I create another integer variable let's say number of oranges.
And let's say this is seven.
All I have to do is after number of apples place down a comma again and this time give number of oranges.
And now number of oranges will be displayed after the second format specifier right here.
And the order here is important.
So the first format specifier will be replaced by the first argument here after the text.
And the second one right here will be replaced by the second one.
Let's go ahead and test this out as well.
Let's save it.
Let's compile it.
Play the game and you can see that we are printing seven after ten, and the seven is the number of
oranges integer variable we created.
Good.
Like I mentioned percentage D is for printing an integer variable inside of our text.
We also have two other format specifiers for floats and strings.
Printing out a floating point number is going to be extremely similar to what we did here, and that
is why I'm going to give that to you as a challenge.
```

### Operator Precedence {#operator-precedence}

```
Hello and welcome.
In this lecture we're going to learn about operator precedence.
Let's go ahead and get started.
So in the last lecture we learned how to do addition subtraction multiplication and division.
But we only did a single operation inside of an expression.
So that's the first thing I want to cover.
And with that the topic of operator precedence is going to come into play.
So first of all, I'm going to get rid of these lines at the top.
So we're going to focus on this calculation right here.
And you can see that we are making a division.
Like I said we're not limited to a single operation in an expression.
For example, after the division I could say something like let's add ten to this.
And then after that we'll take 3.5 off.
So now we have an expression with three operations a division and addition and subtraction.
But like I said with this, the question comes into mind which of these operations will be done first?
Which will be done second?
Which will be done third in C plus plus.
The real life mathematical rules apply, so multiplication and division will be done first.
And after that addition and subtraction will be done.
In this case this division will be done first.
That will be like 2.4.
And then we will add ten to this.
And then take 3.5 off.
So 12.4 here.
And then what is that 8.9.
Quick maths.
So let's compile that and see if that's accurate.
But like I said the real life mathematical rules apply division and multiplication done first and everything
else addition and subtraction later.
So let's save this.
Go into unreal.
Compile it.
Let's play the game and you can see that it is indeed 8.9.
Cool.
So let's go back into Visual Studio.
And as I mentioned a second ago, this phenomenon is called operator precedence.
The rules that govern which operation is going to be done first.
These are done from left to right.
So for example, if we had a multiplication here, in this case this division would be done.
That would be 2.4.
After that we would multiply this by ten, which would be 24.
And after that we would take 3.5 off this.
That would be 21.5 I think, or 20.5 rather.
So let's go ahead and compile this.
See that it is indeed the case.
And you can see that it is in fact 20.5.
So the thing I want you to understand is that in an expression with multiple operations.
Multiplication and division will be done before addition and subtraction.
And in the case that you have both, it's going to go from left to right.
So the leftmost operation will be done.
And then it's going to move to the right.
So it will start here and then move to the right.
But of course if there's a multiplication or division on the right side and on the left side, there's
only additions and subtractions.
It's going to start there.
So let me also demonstrate that real quick.
So I'm just going to delete all of this.
Let's say that we have eight plus 2.5 minus one multiplied by ten.
So in this case even though the rule is that we start from the left, since we have a multiplication,
of course we're going to start here.
So some rules kind of overwrite others when it is appropriate.
So that is operator precedence in a nutshell.
And the most important of the operators when it comes to precedence is going to be the parentheses.
With parentheses, we can control what we want to be done first, regardless of multiplication, division,
anything.
So for example, let's actually say three here instead of one.
For example in this calculation right here this will be done first right.
Three times ten.
Because that's a multiplication.
It will be done before these.
If I wanted to do this first.
So for example like I said this is done first.
But let's say I want this to be done first.
I can put a set of parentheses in code, not in a drawing.
So let me actually put them here so I can put these parentheses here.
And this is going to make sure that this calculation is done first regardless of addition subtraction
anything.
There's parentheses over this.
This will be done first.
I'm manually explicitly telling the compiler that do this first before anything.
And after that we can do the multiplication with ten.
So in this case this would be -0.5.
We would multiply this by ten after that because division or multiplication would be higher precedence
than addition.
Here this would be negative five.
And then we would add eight to this.
And that would be three.
So let's compile let's say first compile and test to make sure that I'm not lying to you.
And you can see that it is indeed three.
Okay.
Great.
So that's basically all I wanted to cover.
Now we understand what happens when we have multiple operations in a single expression.
We know that division and multiplication will be done before addition and subtraction.
And if you want to take things into our own hands, we can use parentheses to put anything to the first
spot.
So that's it for this lecture.
Let's continue in the next one.
```

### Member Variables {#member-variables}

```
Hello and welcome.
In this lecture we're going to learn about member variables, which are variables that belong to a certain
class.
Before we do that though, let's talk about the variables that we created so far.
Those were called local variables.
They're called local variables because they can only be accessed in the function they were created in.
In this case we were working in the Beginplay function, and the variables we created inside of that
are local to begin play.
So that means we can't access these in other functions, which kind of makes them limiting in a way.
Another limitation of local variables is that they will get deleted at the end of the function they
were created in.
In this case, my integer here and my floats will get deleted at the end of Beginplay.
Because of these reasons, they don't make a good choice for saving information long term.
That is when member variables come into play.
These are declared in the header file and these are going to retain their data.
They're not going to get deleted at the end of a function.
We can also access these in all of the functions of the class.
They're not local to a specific function.
They're accessible in all of the functions of that class.
So this is an example of declaring a member variable inside of a class.
In the Unreal Engine we have the option to register a member variable using the U property macro.
Once we do this, we can see and edit this variable inside of the editor.
We can use the u property macro just like this inside of the class.
When we declare the variable on top of that, we're going to put u property a set of parentheses.
And then inside of that we're going to tell unreal what we want to do with this variable.
In this case, we want to be able to edit it.
So we're going to give the edit anywhere arguments.
Once we do this we're going to be able to see this my float variable within the Unreal Engine so that
we can edit it without going back into Visual Studio and without having to compile again.
This is going to make it really easy to change values of variables and tweak our game and fine tune
it.
```

### Structs {#structs}

```
Welcome.
In this lecture we're going to learn about structs in C plus plus.
Let's go ahead and get started.
So first of all what is a struct.
A struct is a group of variables packaged together under one name.
In order to create a struct, we need to use the struct keyword.
After that, we're going to give it a name.
In this case it's called f vector.
Next up we're going to create an opening and closing curly brace along with a semicolon.
In between those curly braces, we're going to create the variables that we want to package together.
In this case we have three floats named x, y, and z.
This would be the declaration of a struct.
It's like the recipe.
After we do this, we can start using the name of the struct in order to create variables.
So for example, I can use the f vector type that we declared here in order to create a variable called
my vector.
This, my vector variable is going to have three floats inside of it called x, y, and z.
And after I declare it like this, I can access those values using the dot operator.
So here I'm accessing the x property of the my vector variable.
And I'm assigning 10.4 to that.
Creating a struct like this makes it really easy to manage our data, because it's all packaged up in
one place.
And it also lets us give meaning to our data as well.
I can name something vector and I know exactly what it is instead of just a bunch of variables.
In this case, f vector is something that's actually defined by unreal.

But if we had to do it, it would look something like this.

Why is this one and z is this one.
In this case, the location vector is used to control the 3D position of an actor.

this.

The constructor for f vector is the same name.
It's called f vector.

And then maybe something like 7.6 for Z.

And then it's going to give it back to us.
And then we're going to use the assignment operator and save that inside of test vector here.
After we create the vector we can access its variables by using the dot operator just like we saw in
the slideshow.
So I can say test vector dot x and the x has to be capitalized to access the x property of this vector
just like that.
And I can do the same with y and z.
Now I can use these values inside of expressions or I can reassign values to them as well.
So for example I can say test vector dot z is equal to 2.5.
Initially z was 7.6.
And now on this line we assigned 2.5 to it.
So this is how to create an effector variable and then how to access or reassign its properties.
This is going to be extremely useful because a lot of things in unreal like location rotation scale,
all of those are effectors.
And if you want to start changing them we're going to have to use structs.
So this is the first step to doing those operations which are going to be very cool.
And now for your challenge I want you to go ahead and create an f vector member variable.
You can call it my vector.
This is going to be a member variable just like I said.
So the one we just created was a local variable inside of Beginplay.
For this one, you're going to go into the header file and create a member variable.

from Visual Studio.
Pause the video, give the challenge a go, and come back when you're done.
Okay.
So we can get rid of the test vector.
Or we can just leave it here for now.
And then inside of the header file at the bottom we're going to create a new member variable which is
going to be an effector.
The name is going to be my vector.
We're going to give it a default value.
So let's use the assignment operator, the constructor, which is the same name as the struct type effector,
but with a set of parentheses to turn it into a function call.
```

### Tick {#tick}

```
Hello and welcome.
In this lecture we're going to make our moving platform move continuously using the tick function.
Let's go ahead and get started okay.
So we learned how to set the location of our moving platform using the set actor location function.
And now we can practically make it move anywhere we want at the start of the game.
It's going to happen at the start of the game, because we are doing this inside of Beginplay right
here.
But this isn't the final effect we want.
We want to be able to move the moving platform continuously so that we can jump on it and go to different
places.
In order to make this happen, we're going to learn about this function called tick, which is right
here inside of moving platform, underneath the Beginplay function.
In order to understand how this function works, we're going to dive into the topic of a game loop.
So all games and game engines use what is called a game loop in order to function.
This is going to be a loop that runs continuously and update the game.
Usually it looks something like this.
First of all, the game or game engine is going to get input from the player.
You can think of this as the keyboard.
It can be the mouse or it can be a controller.
Once the input is handled, the engine or the game is going to update the game state.
You can think of this as setting variables, calling functions, making things happen in the game,
and after that the game is going to render graphics to the screen as an image.
So this is what a typical game loop looks like.
And since it is a loop, this will be done over and over again.
The game is going to get input, update the game state, Render the graphics input.
Update render.
Input update.
Render.
You can almost think of this as a flipbook, but it's interactive.
And in this example of a flipbook, each paper in the flipbook would be a frame of the game.
So you can think of one cycle of the game loop here as a frame.
Let's take a look at another visualization of the game loop.
So first of all we are going to get the input in this case from a keyboard.
Then we're going to update the state.
In this case we're making a call to set actor location.
But this can be any piece of code that makes things happen in the game.
And finally we are displaying the graphics on the screen.
And we're going to do this over and over again in a loop.
So in a nutshell that is the game loop and how it works.
Each cycle of that game loop is called a frame.
Like I just said, your computer or your CPU is going to try to run that loop as fast as it can.
So in some cases, it can run the game loop hundreds of times per second.
There's a special term for the amount of frames you have in one second, and it's called FPS or frames
per second.
This is going to be dependent on the speed of your CPU.
You might be running a game at 30 fps or 60 or more.
Like I said, depending on the speed of your CPU.
Now we can get back to the tick function.
This is a special function that is called every frame of the game.
So if you have 60 frames a second, your CPU is running the game at 60.
Tick is being called in each one of those frames.
So the function we've been working with so far was beginplay.
This is only going to get called once when the actor gets into the game or when the game is started.
After that, the actor is going to start running the tick function, and this will be done over and
over again, possibly up to 6070 times per second, depending on your FPS.
So it makes it.
This quality of tick makes it the perfect place to make things move or make things happen over time.
Because again, tick is being called all the time continuously until the character or until the actor
is deleted or the game is over.
Now let's go back into Visual Studio and start using the tick function.
So first of all, I'm going to place a log statement inside of the tick function so that we can better
understand how it is being called.
Let's create a new log call with the usual arguments log temp display.
And then for the text we'll say tick is called.
Before when we were making log statements in begin play.
They were only being printed once at the start of the game.
Now, since we're making this call in tick, it is going to get printed every frame, so multiple multiple
times per second.
Let's save this.
Go into unreal, compile it.
And when we play the game you will see that the output log is flooded with tick is called messages.
These are being printed continuously which is kind of difficult to see right now.
So we can actually right click on this hit clear log.
And immediately almost instantly it is flooded again with tick is called logs.
Okay now that's a little bit more clear.
Let's actually make our moving platform move by making the set actor location call inside of tick instead
of Beginplay.
We're going to cut this from here with Ctrl X or Ctrl x.
Paste it down here with Ctrl V.
And now we're making the set actor location called in tick.
But this isn't enough to make it move.
Because although we are making this call over and over again, we're doing it with the same vector.
We're setting it to the same position over and over again, which isn't going to look different.
In order to fix the problem, we need to increase the properties of my vector inside of tick as well.
So on the line before set actor location, we're going to increase my vectors Z float.
We're going to access that by using the dot operator.
And we're going to assign the same value which is my vector dot z again plus one.
This is going to increase the z position of our moving platform by one each frame.
So let me explain how this works.
Let's say that at the moment my vector z is ten.
We're going to take that ten.
In this expression we're going to add one to it.
So it's going to be 11.
And then we're going to set that back into my vector z.
So whatever value we have inside of it we're going to add one and then assign it back to my vector z.
And we're going to continue doing this each frame.
So from ten it's going to be 11.
From 11 it's going to be 12.
And so far and so forth, each frame increasing it by one centimetre.
This is going to make our platform move up because we're increasing its z position.
And of course after we increase it we're calling set actor location with the new and increased vector
so that we actually set the location.
So that is how this is working.
Let's save this.
Go into unreal.
Compile it.
When we play the game, the platform should be going upwards.
We're going to have to look that way.
And you can see that it is going up.

And I'm going to move it up there so that we can actually be closer to it.
Just like that.

the z.

And it's going to take us to a different location.
```

### Comments {#comments}

```
Hello and welcome.
In this lecture we're going to learn about comments in C plus plus.
Let's go ahead and get started.
Okay.
So first of all what is a comment.
A comment is a piece of code that is ignored by the compiler.
In order to create a comment, you need to place down two forward slashes just like this.
And then everything that you type after that.
Is going to be ignored by the compiler.
So essentially you can type anything you want here with the two forward slashes.
This is only going to turn that line into a comment.
We have another type of comment which is created with a forward slash and then an asterisk.
And then you need to place down a closing asterisk and forward slash.
With this one you can create some new lines and everything in between these two will be a comment.
So with this approach you can create multi-line comments.
Both of these will be fully ignored by the compiler.
So why do we need comments?
First of all, they are very good.
If you want to leave yourself some notes inside of your code, we have some comments here automatically
created by unreal and these are explaining what the functions do.
For example, beginplay is called when the game starts or when the actor is spawned.
And tick here is called every frame.
So unreal is being nice.
And it's explaining to you what these functions do.
And you can do the same thing for yourself, especially as a beginner.
This is very good because you can leave some comments.
And even if you forget what the code did, you can remind yourself what it does.
So take a look at all of these comments, read them and see how they are done.
Because I'm going to give you a challenge to create your own comments.

there.

We can say increase x position by one each frame.
I'm going to add each frame because we're doing this in tech.
If you wrote the same comment, good job.
If you didn't just copy mine, if yours was not really accurate.
But this is how to leave a comment for yourself so that you know what a piece of code is doing.
So this is the first way you can use comments.
And another way I use all the time is to disable a piece of code, since a comment is going to get ignored.
We can use it to disable a line of code.
So for example, here I can put two forward slashes and set active location is going to turn into a
comment and it won't be called anymore.
This is good when you want to test something really quick, but you don't want to delete a certain line
of code.
You can just turn it into a comment, do your test, and then come back and delete the comment and your
code is back working as intended.
You can also use the other type of comment to do the same thing as well.
If you want to disable a big chunk of code.
So that is what a comment is.
It's not that complicated compared to what we learned so far, but I thought I'd teach you this and
get it out of the way.
```

### Delta Time {#delta-time}

```
Hello and welcome to another lecture.
In this one, we're going to make our game frame rate independent by using the Deltatime variable.
Let's go ahead and get started okay.
So now that we made the moving platform move by increasing its position inside of the tick function,
each frame, we're going to start hitting a problem.
I'm calling that problem the speed problem.
This has to do with the different speeds different computers are going to run our game adds.
For example, a fast computer might be running it at 100 frames per second, and a slower one might
be running it at 30 frames per second.
This is going to cause problems if we are adding or subtracting a value inside of the tick function.
And that is exactly what we're doing right now.
We're taking current location x, we're increasing it by one, and then we're assigning it back each
frame because we're doing it inside of tick.
So if we have 100 fps, that means in one second the platform is going to move 100 units or 100cm.
But on the other hand, if we have 30 frames per second, it's only going to move 30cm.
That means our game is going to behave differently on different computers, which is very bad.
In order to fix this, we use a special variable called delta time.
This is the amount of time it took the CPU to compute the last frame of the game.
We learned that the game loop looks something like this.
Get the input, update the game state, and render graphics.
Doing all of this is essentially computing one frame of the game, and the time it takes in seconds
to compute this for the last frame is called Delta time.
So whenever we have an operation like that inside of tick, we can multiply the value we're adding with
delta time in order to make our code frame rate independent, which means our code is going to run the
same independent of the frame rate.
This is how that is going to look.
You can see that delta time is actually an argument of this function.
And this is going to be given to us as an input by unreal itself.
So we don't need to worry about calculating the delta time ourselves.
It is given to us as an input to this function.
So we can go ahead and use this delta time variable here.
In this case we were adding one to the current location x.
So we have to multiply one with delta time.
And I placed some parentheses here to make it clear.
Now I'm going to explain how delta time works in more detail.
Just a heads up.
Delta time is a little bit of an advanced concept.
And at this point you're simply just learning the basics of C plus plus.
So if it doesn't make sense, don't worry.
When I was learning this ten years ago for the first time, it didn't make sense to me.
Initially, you need to give these concepts some time to sit inside of your head, and one day it's
just going to click.
Feel free to watch this video again and again.
Visit it again in the future as well.
Just know that Delta time is something we use in order to make operations like these frame rate independent.
So here is the line of code that's giving us problems.
Now we're going to take a look at two different computers.
One is slow and this one is running at ten frames per second and one is fast.
And this one is running at 100 frames per second.
So the slow one can only compute ten frames in a second.
And that means each frame is taking 0.1 seconds to compute, which also makes the delta time 0.1.
In the case of the faster computer, it can compute many more frames, and that means each frame is
taking much less time, in this case 0.01 seconds.
So you can immediately see the slower a computer is, the higher the delta time, and the faster a computer
is, the smaller the delta time.
This is the magic number that's going to make things equal right now inside of this calculation we're
not using delta time.
So on the slow computer in one second we would increase the position by ten centimeters.
Because we have ten frames.
And each frame we're increasing it by one.
So one times ten is ten.
On the fast computer we have ten times more frames.
So 100 frames moving by one each frame.
Which means we're going to move by 100cm in one second.
So this is the problem I was just talking about the slow computer.
And the fast computer is going to run the game at different speeds.
Let's take a look at what's going to happen when we introduce Delta time into these equations.
Slow computer had a delta time of 0.1.
We're going to multiply the equation with 0.1.
And that is going to give us one.
The fast computer had a delta time of 0.01.
We're also going to include that for this equation.
And that's also going to give us one.
So you can see that when we multiply these two equations with their respective delta times, they produce
the same number.
Now in both cases, the platform is only going to move by one centimeter.
You can change these numbers.
For example you can change one here with a different number in order to make the platform go at a different
speed.
Put that number into these equations instead of one and you will see that it will work the same.
This works because the slower a computer gets, the delta time is going to get larger to make up for
that speed loss inside of this equation.
And in the same way, the faster a computer gets, the delta time is going to get smaller in order to
make up for that extra speed.
So by using Delta time, we can make sure that we're going to move the moving platform at our desired
speed, regardless of frame rate.
Literally, a computer could be running at one frame per second or a million frames per second And it
wouldn't matter because we're using delta time.
One thing to keep in mind is that as soon as we start using Delta time, you can see that the numbers
went down drastically.
So before we were moving ten centimeters and now we're only moving by one.
And on the fast computer we were actually moving 100cm.
And now we're down to one centimeter a second.
So what we need to do is we need to start thinking in terms of how many centimeters we want to move
in a single second and change this value based on that.

And that's going to mean we want this to move at 100cm per second.
So that is how delta time works.

And then we're going to multiply one by delta time.
And that is going to make our code frame rate independent.
We can save this go into Visual Studio compile it.
Nothing is going to change.
So don't get excited for this.

But this is something very important that we need to do.
Because the last thing that you want to happen is send your game to a friend, and it doesn't work as
```

```
And now we have these member functions that are going to be responsible for moving and in the future
rotating the platform.
And when we take a look at tick we can see that that is happening.
We can easily read this function now.
And if you want to see more details we can take a look at the functions individually.
So that's it.
For this lecture we learned how to do member functions in C plus plus.
```

### Classes And Instances {#classes-and-instances}

```
Welcome.
In this lecture, we're going to do a deep dive into classes and learn what an instance is.
Let's go ahead and get started.
So in C plus Plus and Unreal classes and instances go hand in hand.
In order to understand instances we need to understand a little bit more about classes.
So what is a class?
The class is what we created here.
Inside of the header file.
You can think of the class as a recipe that describes how to create a moving platform.
It shows us what type of data the moving platform has with the variables.
It also shows us what type of functionality a moving platform has with the functions.
And we know that the actual definitions of those functions are in the C plus plus file.
So together the cpp and the header file creates the class A recipe that describes how to create a moving
platform.
An instance, on the other hand, is the actual game object.
Unreal creates inside of the level.
In this case, we only have a single instance right now in the level created from the moving platform
class, this one right here.
So like I just said, it's a good idea to think of the class as a recipe.
And then we use that recipe to create a dish.
And the real power of this system comes into play when we start creating multiple instances.
We have the recipe, and now we can create as many of these instances as we want.
In our case, we created the recipe for a moving platform.
Now we can create infinite amounts of them, and they're all going to have the same variables and the
same functionality as our recipe as our class.
So this is a good analogy to adapt.
Let's take a look at more realistic visualizations for classes.
Here we have the class moving platform.
It has some data, some variables, and some functions.
We know that it looks like this inside of our code.
We're going to use this to create a couple of instances here.
And the instances are going to look something like this.
In the level, what I want you to understand here is that the instances at the start are identical to
the class.
So they're going to start with the default values we have in the recipe or in the class.
In this case platform velocity is 100.
Even though it's actually a vector, let's just say it's a float for the sake of example.
And we have another one called move Distance here.
And this is 200 both floats.
When we create an instance at the start it's going to have these values.
But after that the instances are free to change those default values.
In this case this first instance has changed the platform velocity to be 200 and the move distance to
be 400.
So it's completely different from the original class.
But the changes an instance makes doesn't change the class.
You can make a dish according to the recipe, and then maybe you can add a little bit of an extra ingredient
inside of that, but that is not going to change the original recipe.
In the second instance here, the platform velocity is the default value.
So that was unchanged.
But the move distance is.
So in summary the changes you make to an instance doesn't change the class, but the changes made to
the class will change all of the instances.
For example, if I create a new function or a new variable inside of a class, all of the instances
will automatically receive that update.
So that's an important rule for you to know.
Class changes will change instances, but an instance change won't change the class or it won't change
other instances either.
This one setting platform velocity to 200 won't change the other instance.
That will still be the default value.
So let's actually take a look at our instance that we have in the game right now, because this one
has a customized platform velocity.
As you can see, it's set to be -100 on the x.
And we know that the default value was zero.
Now let's go ahead and create another instance.
And one important thing I want you to notice is that this cube component that we created here, this
was created in the instance.
This change will not affect other instances.
So if we go into the content browser, into cplusplus classes, obstacle assault.
And if we drag in dragon, a new instance of a moving platform.
You will see that that one doesn't have a cube and therefore it doesn't have a transform yet.
We need to manually go into the details again and create a cube here.
And then we need to do all the scaling again.
And that is because like I said this one right here, the cube was added to the instance.
So new instances created from the moving platform class here are not affected by that change.
Let's place this over here.
And let's take a look at the platform velocity of this new instance.
You will see that it is set to the default value of 000.
This again is the same rule.
The change we made to this instance the instance, the -100 is not carried to other instances.
So this new one is set to the default value.
And we can customize this as well.
We can set it to be 50 on the Y for example, which is going to make it go that way.
I also want to show you that a change that we're going to make to the class will be carried to all of
the instances.
So in this case, I'm just going to create a test variable here.
We'll make it a uproperty.
We'll make it a vector.
We'll essentially just copy and paste this one and call it test vector.
I will delete this later and we'll set the defaults to be one, one one.
Just so it is different than this one.
We can save this.
Compile it I'm just going to use live coding because this is just a test.
We're going to delete it.
Otherwise I would have done it from Visual Studio.
Now if we take a look at these instances we will see that both of them will have a test vector.
Here is the one for moving platform two the new one.
And when we click on the old one, you can see that this one also has a test vector.
So this is a powerful system and it's important for you to understand how it works.
We created two instances from the same class moving platform.
After that we made a change to the moving platform class.
We created a vector inside of it.
After we compiled that change was carried to the two instances we have in the game, and if we had 100,
all of them would have received the update.
But on the other hand, the individual changes we make to the instances don't affect others.
The cube we created in the first one did not carry over to the new one, so we had to create it again
essentially.
And also the changes we make to the variables are also unique to that instance.
Now I'm going to give you a challenge.
I want you to go ahead and create a new moving platform instance in the level, just like I did.
I want you to create a cube.
Or you can also use a different shape component inside of it.
Scale it.
Make it look nice.
And finally I want you to customize its platform velocity.
Pause the video, give it a go, and come back when you're done.
All right.
I did this already with the second one, so I'm not going to do it again.
But good job if you did the challenge.
Now essentially we understand what a class is.
It's like the recipe and we understand what an instance is.
It's the actual dish that we create using the recipe.
And we also understand how these are connected to each other and how the changes we make affect these
instances.
That's it for this lecture.
We'll continue in the next one.
```

### Distance Between Vectors {#distance-between-vectors}

```
Hello and welcome.
In this lecture, we're going to start keeping track of how many centimeters a platform has moved.
We're also going to learn how to use the scope resolution operator in a different setting.
Let's go ahead and get started okay.
So at the moment when we start the game, our platforms are moving in a certain direction and they never
stop.
This isn't exactly what we're trying to achieve here.
We want a certain platform to start moving up until a certain distance, and then come back.
In order to make this happen, we need to do a bunch of different operations.
But first of all, we need to start keeping track of the starting location of the platform.
Let's call this SL we're going to get this by calling Get Actor location at the start of the game.
And we're going to save this in a member variable.
The next step is going to be calculating the distance between the start location and the current location
of the platform.
For example, let's say that the platform came all the way here.
This is the current location.
What is the distance between the start location and this one?
We're going to call that the distance moved.
And we're also going to save this in a member variable.
Once the distance moved reaches a certain threshold let's say here the platform is going to come back.
In this lecture we're going to focus on these two.
The start location and the distance moved.
So let's go into Visual Studio.
And we're going to create that start location effector.
And that is going to be your challenge.
Go ahead and create an effector member variable called start location.
You don't need to use your property for this.
We're not going to register it with unreal.
We're only going to use this in C plus plus.
You also don't need to give this a default value after you create it.
Call get actor location in Begin play and assign the return value to the start location.
Pause the video, give it a go, and come back when you're done.
All right, inside of the header we'll create a couple of new lines down here.
And we're going to create an F factor called start location.
Just like I said no need for a new property or a default value.
And the reason we don't want a default value is that by default, we don't know where a platform is.
Only when the game starts we will know where it is.
And we know that begin play is the place where the game starts or where the actor is spawned.
So inside of here we're going to set start location.
We're going to assign the return value of get actor location so that when the game starts we're keeping
track of that original location of the moving platform.
That's going to be important because ultimately, just like I explained, we want to calculate that
distance and we also want to come back to that original start location.
And now that we have the start location, we can calculate the distance between the current location.
We're going to do that inside of move platform right after we make the set actor location call, because
we know that after we make this call, we are at a different location.
The newest, most up to date location of the platform.
And in order to calculate the distance, we're going to use a function from the f vector class.
Classes like f vector have functions inside of them that we can use.
And in order to access those functions, we can use the scope resolution operator.
This is another use case for this operator.
Once I place this down we get this box right here, which is going to show us a list of all the functions

called v1 and one called V2.
Don't worry about these const keywords.
Don't worry about this ampersand.
Basically, ignore every keyword that you don't know and we're only going to focus on the parameter
list for now.
This tells us we need to pass two points.
The first one obviously is going to be the start location, and the second one will be the current location.
You can also see the parameters down here.
And the return type is going to be a double.
And we know that a double is just a float with more precision.
So we can just save the return value in a float.
No problem.
The description here is also helpful.
It says that this gives us the distance between two points, and the return value is the distance between
two points.
So basically we need to give this function our points.
And then it's going to return to us the distance between them as a double which is also basically a
float.
So while we have this selected we can just hit enter and then make the function call inside of the parentheses.
We'll pass the start location and the current location.
Which we have access to because the current location is created just above this function call.
So this is how to make the function call.
And now we need to save the return value in a variable so that we can actually use it.

anywhere.
```

### If Statements {#if-statements}

```
Welcome to another lecture.
In this one, we're going to learn how to use an if statement and make our platform go back.
So at the moment we are keeping track of the starting location of the moving platform.
And as it is going forward, we are calculating the distance moved.

This is going to be the distance threshold that we want the platform to reach before sending it back.
This is going to be a float.

Let's also say that 300 is somewhere here compared to the starting location.
Once the platform reaches here, the distance moved is going to be equal to 300, or it's going to be
greater than 300.
In that case, we're going to send it back.

is greater than or equal to the move distance.
That means we have reached or surpassed the threshold.

operators.

is true or false.

In that case this will be true.

Is five equal to ten?
No it's not.
```
