### Overshoot Problem {#overshoot-problem}

```
Hello and welcome.
In this lecture we're going to talk about the overshoot problem of our platform movement code.
Let's go ahead and get started.
So first of all I'm going to delete these extra platforms so that we can just focus on a single one.
And let me explain you what the problem is.
At the moment we are keeping track of the start location and then we're making the platform move.
And once it reaches the move distance, we're making it go back.
The problem is we're checking for greater than or equal to with the distance moved and move distance,
which means there is a chance that the distance moved will be actually greater than the move distance.
So let's say the move distance is 300.
There's a chance that we moved slightly beyond it and we're at 301, which means the platform actually
moved beyond.
It's just like I said, the problem with this is that we're setting the start location to the current
location inside of the if statement, which means this new location will be the start location instead
of the correct end position.
Now this difference will be very small.
This will be like 0.01cm or something very small, and you're not going to be able to see this in the
game.
But if you let the game run for a couple of hours, these 0.1cm will add up and eventually the platform
will lose its original course, which is something very, very bad that we don't want to happen in the
game.
In order to fix this, we're going to precisely calculate the end position instead of using the current
location of the platform.
In order to make this precise calculation, we need to use the move distance multiplied with the direction
of the platform.
And then we're going to add this to the start location to this one the original start location.
This is going to give us the precise end point.
And then we're going to use that precise endpoint to set the start location.
Once the platform reaches the end and now is going to go back, this is going to make our movement code
bulletproof.
And this will never go off its course.
In this lecture we're only going to demonstrate this overshoot problem.
And in the next one we're going to implement the solution.
In order to see the overshoot problem, we can actually calculate how many centimeters we went beyond
the move distance.
We're going to do that inside of the if statement.
We know what the distance moved is, which is the actual distance we moved.
And we know what the move distance is, which is what we should have moved.
And the overshoot is simply going to be subtracting the move distance from the distance moved.
I'm going to give this to you as a challenge.
Go ahead and make that calculation.
I just told you you're going to subtract the move distance from the distance moved.
And then I want you to save that in a variable which is going to be a float.

Pause the video, give it a go and come back when you're done.

Okay, give it a go.
All right.
Let's create a new float here.
We're going to call this overshoot.
And we're going to assign this to distance moved minus move distance which is going to give us the overshoot
distance.
And after that we're going to log this using log so that we can actually see it.
Display text.
And in the text I am going to print first of all the name of this actor.
We'll place down a percentage s for the string.

and the game will just look different.

### Correcting Overshoot {#correcting-overshoot}

```
Hello and welcome.
In this lecture, we're going to fix the overshoot problem and make the movement code bullet proof.
Let's go ahead and get started.
So in the previous lecture we determined that we were moving the platform a little bit too far.
And we were actually missing the movement distance we were aiming.
This difference was very small, something like 0.01cm.
But we determined that if we let the game play for a long enough time period, these are going to add
up and the original course of the platform will be distorted.
In order to fix this, we're going to calculate the precise spot that we need to stop at.
And we're going to use that as the start location, because currently we're just taking this wrong location
as the new start location.
And that is making our course ever so slightly wrong.
Each time we do it.
This will be the new start location when we come here, and we will go back from this point.
So let's take a look at the code, and let's try to understand why this problem was happening again.
You can see that right here inside of the if statement.
We're setting the start location to the current location.
But right now we know that this current location is overshooting by this amount.
So it is not correct.
What we need to do is instead of using this, we are going to calculate the correct distance right here
before we set the platform velocity to be negative.
In order to calculate this we need to get the direction of the platform.
We know that the platform velocity is what gives us the direction, but the platform velocity also has
a magnitude.
In this case we're only interested in the direction of this vector.
So we're going to get the normalized version of it.
The normalized version of a vector is the same vector with the length of one.
So it's still going to give us the direction without any of the magnitude.
We're not going to get into the details of the vector math.
Just know that we are going to get the direction out of this platform velocity vector.
And the way we do that is by accessing one of its functions.
We can do that by using the dot operator.
The name of the function is going to be get save normal and it's not going to accept any arguments.
This function is going to return the normalized version of the platform velocity, which is going to
give us the direction.
So let's save it inside of a new variable that we're going to call move direction.
You might be asking why did we use the dot operator.
Because up here we were using the scope resolution operator to access the dist function.
There is a difference between this one and this one right here.
When we wanted to access dist we did it using the F vector class.
When you want to access a class function, you need to use the scope resolution operator.
On the other hand, platform velocity right here is an instance.
It's an instance of the f vector class.
When you want to access a function that's inside of an instance, you use the dot operator instead.
So that is the difference there.
And now we have the direction that we need to use when we make the calculation.
Next up we're going to create a new vector called new start location.
And this is going to be the start location.
Plus the move direction times the move distance.
We're going to take the old start location as our starting point.
And we're going to increase it by the movement direction times the movement distance, which is going
to give us that precise end points that we need to stop at.
Next up we're going to call set actor location with the new start location so that we actually correct
the overshoot we had.
After we do that, we can also set the start location to be this new start location as well.
Only after we do this we can set the velocity to be it's negative.
Make sure to do this at the end.
If you do it before what we did here, it's not going to work.
Let's save this.
Go into unreal.
Compile it.
Now when we play the game it's not really going to be visible.
But every time we change direction we are actually correcting that overshoot.
And this way our movement code is bulletproof and it is never going to leave its original course.
So if what we did in here didn't make complete sense to you, don't worry, there is some vector math
here that is involved, especially with the normal.
Just know that now we're using the correct location for the end point.
Instead of using the current location which had the overshoot.
We're going to leave it here for this lecture and continue in the next one.
```

### Function Return Values {#function-return-values}

```
Welcome.
In this lecture we're going to learn about the return values of functions.
Let's get started.
Okay so we learned a lot about functions.
And the return values are like the final piece of this puzzle.
Every function in C plus plus has potential to return a single value.
The type of this value is written in the function signature before the name of the function.
In this case, this function is going to return an integer.
And once you specify a data type here, you have to return a value using the return keyword.
In this case, this function is returning a variable called my integer, which is set to be ten.
Up here.
Here is a list of different types of values we can have as a return type.
Of course, we can use the basic C plus plus types, but we can also use structs or classes.
We are not limited to just f string or f factor.
I just written these here as examples.
We can use any class or any struct.
Void is a little bit special.
It means no return value.
So if you use void as the return type here you don't have to use return.
But if you use anything else, you have to use return.
Otherwise your code isn't going to compile.
This return value can be a variable like the one I have here.
It can be straight up a number like ten.
I could have written ten here, or it can also be an expression that evaluates to the return type.
The way we make a call, a function call with a function that returns a value looks like this.
You are going to make the function call as usual, and then you can use this as a value.
In this case we're making the function call.
This is going to return ten.
And then we're going to save ten into the result variable right here.
We can also use a function that returns a value inside of an expression.
In this case, we're making the function call.
This is going to return ten.
And then we're going to multiply that by two.
And then save it in this variable.
So essentially you can just make the function call and use it as a value in any type of expression you
want.
And that's it.
This is how to use return values in functions.
First of all you need to specify it before the function name.
And after that in the function body you have to use the return keyword with a proper value.
One very important thing to know about the return keyword is that once you make this return call, the
function will be over.
So if you have code after the return statement, that code is not going to get called.
Now let's go into Visual Studio and start using this.

Let me also elaborate on what I was saying just a second ago.

Anything after the return is gone, because return is basically returning from the function.
It's ending the function and it's returning the value.
Keep that in mind and usually you're not going to do something like this anyways.
Return value will usually be at the end of the function.

### Movement & Physics {#movement-physics}

```
Hello and welcome.
In this lecture, we're going to learn how to customize the player's movement and how to use Unreal
Engine's built in physics system.
Let's go ahead and get started.
So first of all, we are going to customize the player's movement so that we can control things like
how high the player jumps, how fast the player runs, and a bunch of different things that we can change.
These are going to be important because these are going to determine how we design the level.
And if you try to change these, after you design the level, you are definitely going to break the
level design.
Because for example, let's say that you place these platforms like this.
Let me actually take the platform and you expect the player to jump from here to here, maybe with the
current jump height that's possible, but if you decrease the jump height, then this jump is not going
to be possible and you're effectively breaking the entire level design at that point.

### Packaging The Game {#packaging-the-game}

```
Welcome.
In this lecture, we're going to learn how to package the game up so that we can share it with other
people.
Let's go ahead and get started.
So in order to package the game up, we need to go into this menu right here that says platforms.
Here we have a list of different operating systems.
In this case we're going to go to the windows menu right here and inside of here.
If we click on Package Project that's going to package the game up.
Before we do that though, we can take a look at the configuration.
By default it is set to be developments, which is good to use if you're testing the game yourself.
But if the testing is over and you want to actually ship the game or send it to someone else, you should
click on shipping.
Beneath this we have the architecture, but we don't really need to get into this.
We can just leave it as the project default.
Once everything is selected, we can click on Package Project.
After you do that, unreal is going to want you to pick a location.
I'm going to create a folder here called build.
This is outside of my project folder.
You can also create this inside of the actual project.
In fact, there's a folder here called build.
You can just save it here, but sometimes I like to create it outside of it as well.
So it's just up to you.
We're going to select this build folder as the location.
And after you select it unreal is going to start the packaging process.
The first time you do this it might take a little bit of time, but after that it's going to speed up.
And as you can see it is complete.
Once it is complete, we can go into that folder that we called build.
Inside of that you're going to find a folder called windows.
And this is the final packaged game.
Inside we have the exe file along with the other files generated by unreal.
And in order to play the game we can just basically double click on the exe.
If you get this alert you can just click on Allow Access.
It's fine.
And now you can play the game as a standalone executable, which is kind of cool.
One important thing to keep in mind is to rebuild the game from Visual Studio before you package it.
If you've been using live coding, for example, just make sure to rebuild from the editor.
The Visual Studio, I mean, before you package.
Otherwise you might not get some of the stuff that you did in the final packaged game.
And this is how to package the game up and play it.
And since we didn't, um, include a menu or anything like that, we're going to have to hit alt F4
to exit the game.
If you want to send the game to a friend or someone else, you can right click on this windows folder.
Maybe you should also name it as well before you do it.
Maybe a name like Obstacle Assault is more fitting than windows.
You can right click on it and go to Send To and then click on compressed zipped folder.
That's going to create a zip file out of the project.
And then you can easily send that over to a friend so that they can play the game as well.
So that is how to package the game up so that we can share it with the world.
We're going to leave it here for this lecture.
In fact, this is going to be the final lecture of Obstacle Assault.
It's been a great game.
In the next lecture we're going to do a small wrap up.
And after that we're going to move on to the next project.
See you in the next video.

### Lighting Setup {#lighting-setup}

```
Hello and welcome.
In this lecture we're going to learn how to do the lighting setup for the empty level we created.
Let's go ahead and get started okay.
So first of all let's go back into the lit view mode.
And once we do that you can see that the entire world is black.
That is because we don't have any lights in the scene lighting up the actors, so we can't really see
anything.
Let's fix that first.
To do that, we need to go into the quickly add to the project menu into lights.
And in here we have an actor called a Directional light.
Let's bring this into the world.
And as soon as we do that, you can start seeing the ground plane again.
You can think of the directional light as the sun that is lighting up the game world.
It's infinitely far away, and it's lighting up everything from the same angle.
For this actor, the location doesn't really matter.
We usually just leave it at 000 so we can go ahead and reset its location.
And the one that is important is going to be the rotation of this directional light.
We're going to explore that in just a second.
But before we do that let's create the sky so that we have a nice blue sky instead of this black void.
To do that, we need to go into the visual effects menu.
And we need to bring in a sky atmosphere actor.
Once we do that, we can now see this beautiful blue sky, as you can see.
And if you actually look up, you can see the sun in the sky.
The position of the sun is determined by the rotation of the directional light.
So while I have the directional light selected, if I change the y rotation, you can see that the sun
is moving.
And it's the same with the z rotation.
And the rotation of this directional light is going to determine the type of light we have inside of
the scene.
So it's going to be important.
Next up let's create the bottom half of the sky.
Because you can see that the bottom half is completely black still.
So we need to include another visual effect actor, which is called an exponential height fog.
Let's bring this in as well.
And now we have the bottom half of the sky, which is looking kind of good with these actors.
Location doesn't matter just like the directional light.
So for both of these we can just reset them to be 000.
Awesome.
We're starting to get a sky and atmosphere that is looking good.
Next up we're going to create some clouds.
That is going to be another visual effects actor called Volumetric Cloud.
We can also position this at 000.
And now we have some nice looking clouds in the game world.
Great.
We're almost done.
The final step is bringing in another light actor called skylight.
Let's set this at 000 as well.
And this is a lot more subtle light.
So for example, if we turn it on and off, you can see that it is slightly impacting the lighting.
But it's going to be important as you'll see in just a second.
Now we can play the game and see what it looks like.
And we have this nice looking sky that we're used to from the test levels.

get these shadows being cast over here.
And that is because the sun right now is kind of in front of this cube.
In this angle, you can see that these faces of the cube are much lighter compared to the faces in the
back.
And just like I said a second ago, the shadow is being cast here.
First of all, let's play with the z rotation of the directional light that's going to move the sun
in a circle in the sky.
So while I'm looking at the cube I'm going to play with the Z.
And you can see that as the sun is going in that circle, the angle of the shadow is changing.
And also the faces of the cube that are lit are also changing.
Now that the sun is there, these faces are being lit more and these ones in the back are being darker.
We can still kind of see them.
And that is because of the skylight actually.
So if we go ahead and turn off the skylight, you will see that these faces are going to be much darker
and the shadow is almost pitch black.
So having the skylight, like I said, is important because it gives us that global lighting that is
lighting up the surfaces that aren't being lit at all, or being lit very much by the directional light.
So it's very important.
Next up I'm going to play with the Y Rotation, and that's going to make the sun go down.
In order to create somewhat of a sunrise or sunset effect.
So if I bring it all the way down here, you can see that we get a lot of yellow colors compared to
the blue colors we were getting when the sun was up.
One important thing to realize here is that even though the directional light is now a lot more yellow,
the skylight isn't being updated.
So if you actually turn it off, you can see that the directional light is very yellow.
And when we bring in the skylight, it's kind of adding a blue tint to everything.
In order to update the skylight, we need to click on it.
And we need to.
Click on this recapture button.
And that's going to update the skylight to be the matching color with the directional light.
So keep that in mind as you're updating the directional light.
So for example now we can bring it up in the sky just like that.
You should also go into the skylight and recapture it so that it updates it.
You can also go up in the skylight details here and click on Real Time Capture.
And that way it's going to update immediately without you having to click on recapture.
Great.
So that is the challenge.
I hope you had fun with that.
It's always fun controlling the sun.
Next up I'm going to talk about the mobility settings of these lights.
So if we click on the directional light you will see that the mobility setting is set to be stationary.
So we have three different options here.
The first option is static which means the light can't be changed in the game.
You can't move it.
You can't change its color while the game is running.
You have to do that before you play the game.
This is the fastest option in terms of performance.
And on the other hand, we have movable, which means that the light can be moved and changed during
the game.
It's completely dynamic.
It's the slowest option in terms of performance, but it's the most realistic lighting.
In the middle we have Stationary, which is kind of an in-between setting between static and movable.
You still can't move it, but you can change some settings like the color and the intensity while the
game is running.
By default, this is set to be stationary.
We're going to set it to be movable for some dynamic lighting.
The game that we're going to make isn't going to be huge, so the performance shouldn't really be a
problem for us.
We're also going to do the same for the skylight.
And everything else should also be movable by default.
So no need to change those.
Awesome.
So that is the lighting setup for an empty level.
Finally, let's create a folder for these inside of the outliner so that we can manage them more easily.
Here I'm going to right click create a new folder.
We'll call this the lighting.
And then I'm going to click on the directional light.
Hold down the Ctrl key and then click on the rest of these actors to pick them all.
And then I'll just left click hold and drag them into the lighting folder.
And now we can have them all in one place.
Great.
We're going to leave it here in this lecture and continue in the next one.

### Game Mode {#game-mode}

```
Welcome.
In this lecture, we're going to create a character blueprint and learn about the game mode actor.
Let's go ahead and get started.
So at the moment when we play the game we get the first person character controller, which is great.
But when we run and jump, we can see the hands of the player.
We don't want to have those for the style of game that we're going to build.
We want to have a completely invisible player while the game is running.
If you hit the F8 key, you can actually eject out of the player and you can take a look at the mesh
and you can see that it is literally a robot.
And the game that we're going to build will be in a medieval dungeon.
So this really is not going to fit.
You can even see that there are two meshes on top of each other.
Apparently that was necessary to make this work.
What we want to do is we want to hide both of these meshes.
The character controller came with the first person template.
So the blueprint is inside of the first person blueprints folder right here.
It's called BP underscore first person character.
If you hover over it, you can see that it is inheriting from a class called Dungeon Escape Character.
That one is actually a Cplusplus class.
And if we take a look at Visual Studio in the Solution Explorer, you can see the Dungeon escape character
cpp and dot H right here.
So this is the class that the blueprint is using as the parent.
What we can do is we can create another blueprint child class from this Cplusplus class, or we can
take the blueprint that was created with the template and create a child blueprint of this one.
Yes, you can have a child blueprint of a blueprint.
There's no limit on this.
You can have as many children as you want.
The advantage of that is going to be we're going to get everything from the C plus plus class, and
we're also going to get everything, every modification that was done to the blueprint class.
And we're only going to make our small modifications to that child instead of creating a new blueprint.
And like I said just a second ago, we're simply going to hide those meshes, at least for now.
And I'm going to give this to you as a challenge.
So please go ahead and create a blueprint child class from the BP underscore first person character.
And I want you to call it BP underscore player.
And save this inside of the My Stuff folder.
Next up you're going to open it up and hide the meshes using the visible property.
So there's going to be two meshes inside of the blueprint.
I want you to click on both of them one by one.
Search for the visible property in the details panel and then uncheck it.
Pause the video.
Give the challenge a go and come back when you're done.
All right Let's go into the My Stuff folder.
Right click here.
Click on blueprint class.
And then we're going to do a search for BP underscore first person character.
This one right here.
As you can see it is inheriting from dungeon escape character just like we saw a second ago.
We're going to select this and call it BP Underscore player.
Double click on it to open it up.
And then in here we're going to find the meshes in the components.
Here is the first one.
And here is the second one in the details we'll do a search for visible.
Turn it off.
That's going to get rid of the first one.
And then we'll click on the second one and do the same thing.
And now we have a player controller that doesn't have a visible mesh.
We can also take a look at the other stuff that's inside of here.
First of all we have a camera obviously at the head area to give that first person feel.
There's an arrow component, this one right here.
This just shows us which way the player is facing.
You can't really see this while the game is running.
It's just for debugging purposes in the blueprint.
And then we have a capsule component as the root component.
And this is used with the actual collisions.
So this determines how wide the player is in terms of its collision and everything else.
And finally there is a character movement component which controls the fine details of the movement
of the player.
How much it jumps, how fast it runs, and things like that.
Okay, let's save this, compile it.
And when we run the game now, do you think we're going to see the arms or.
No.
Well the answer is we're still going to get the old first person character because the player starts
right here is programmed to spawn that one.
We need to explicitly tell it to.
Now spawn BP underscore player instead.
That is a rule that we need to change, and that is determined with the game mode actor, which is a
special actor that determines some of the rules of the game.
With the first person template, we actually got one for free.

### Inheritance vs Composition {#inheritance-vs-composition}

```
Hello and welcome.
In this lecture we're going to talk about inheritance and composition.
Let's go ahead and get started okay.
So inheritance and composition these are two ways of designing code.
And so far in the course we have been using inheritance quite a bit.
Inheritance is when a child class automatically has all the functionality of the parent.
The child is a parent.
If we take a look at Visual Studio, we didn't really write any code in this project yet, but we have
this dungeon escape character and if you take a look at the header file, you will see that this class
is inheriting from a character class.
This is how you do inheritance in C plus.
Plus.
By the way, after the class name, you put a colon and then the name of the class that you want to
inherit from.
In this case, dungeon escape character is inheriting from character.
Don't worry about the public here, That's just the mode of the inheritance, but it's not really important
right now.
Inheriting from character means that we have access to all the functions and all the variables that
are inside of the character.
That is how we can make calls inside of our code.
So for example, to functions like let's see.
So here we have add controller your input.
You can see that this is coming from the pawn class.
So it's not exactly the character but pawn is something that the character inherits from.
So it's a big giant inheritance hierarchy.
In fact, if you hold down the control key and click on the character, you can go to that file and
you will see that the character is inheriting from pawn, just like I said.
And if you hold down the control key and go into the pawn and you will see that the pawn is inheriting
from another class.
This whole big inheritance hierarchy is called the Unreal game framework.
And when you use unreal, you have to use it.
But it's not the only way to design code.
There's another way called composition.
Inheritance usually is very rigid and strict.
You can't really break the structure of the hierarchy.
Composition is more relaxed.
It's more of a has a relationship.
So class A has a class B inside of it.
You're essentially breaking up your code into components, and then you're giving them to the classes
as they need them.
So instead of giving you the official description of it, let's try to take a look at an example.
First of all, we'll take a look at an inheritance example.
And let's say that we're trying to build a game with animals.
So you might create a class called animal and create a function called live because all animals need
to live.
That is going to be the top of the hierarchy.
And then you might decide to create some species of animals, starting with mammals.
And as we all know, mammals make milk.
So there's a function in the mammal class called make milk.
And this class is inheriting from animal.
It's a child class of animal.
An animal is the parent.
That means mammal can call the function live as well.
Next up, you say that you want to create some reptiles.
So you create a class.
You create a function called lay eggs, which is something that reptiles can do.
And then you also inherit from animal so that you get access to the live function.
So far, so good.
Next up you decide to create your actual first animal instead of just, you know, types of animals.
And you go with a cow.
You do the logical thing.
You create a class you inherit from mammal because you need to make milk.
By extension, you're also inheriting from animal as well because mammal is inheriting from animal.
So cow can make a call to make milk and also can make a call to live after the cow.
You decide to create a reptile, a snake, and obviously you inherit from reptile so that you can call
lay eggs and also call live as well.
So things are going good.
You created a hierarchy that makes sense.
You're creating these animals and everything is working.
And that is the point.
When everything is nice and organized like this, you might think that inheritance is great, but as
I told you, you're creating a very rigid structure that is going to cause you problems when you have
edge cases.
And the edge case in this case is an animal called platypus.
A platypus is a mammal that can lay eggs.
I bet you didn't expect that one.
And it looks like this.
If you're curious.
So what are you going to do in this case?
You can't inherit from mammal because you need to lay eggs, and you can't inherit from reptile because
you need to make milk.
So this is where inheritance kind of breaks down.
You could create another class oop getaway platypus.
You could create another class called mammal that lays eggs a special case for the platypus.
But that's not really a good solution.
And Cplusplus actually has a functionality called multiple inheritance, so you could, in theory,
inherit from both of these.
But it's not really a good thing to do.
And your code is going to get complicated real quick.
So instead of trying to create this intricate, complicated inheritance hierarchy, we can just turn
to composition using that has a relationship model.
With composition, all we have to do is create a milk making component with the make milk function,
and create an egg laying component with the lay eggs function.
And after that, all we have to do is put a milk making component in the cow, put an egg laying component
in the snake, and then for the platypus, we'll just put one of each.
And then our problem is solved in a much more elegant and simple way.
There are times that composition makes a lot more sense, and there are times you might go with inheritance,
so it's not one or the other.
It comes down to the circumstances.
And like I said, in the case of unreal, since they used inheritance a lot while building the game
framework.
We have to use it nonetheless.
But if you ask my opinion, I'm a fan of composition more so than inheritance, so whenever I can I
try to avoid having inheritance or too much of it in my code.
Okay, let's give you a challenge.
So how could components help here?
Let's say that we're trying to create a piece of code that is going to move an actor.
And in this example right here we have a mover actor class that's inheriting from static mesh actor.
And then we have two more classes here moving door and moving wall.
And these are both inheriting from mover actor.
And that is completely fine because the door and the wall they need to be static mesh actors.
But what if we want to create a moving light?
We can't inherit from this mover actor because we don't want to be a static mesh.
We want to be a light actor.
So think of how we can get rid of the mover actor and use components to fix this problem.
Pause the video.
Maybe get a pen and paper out and think about this issue.
Okay.
Welcome back.
So the solution is going to be like this.
We're going to get rid of the mover actor.
And we're going to directly inherit from static mesh actor for the door and for the wall.
And then for the lights.
We're simply going to inherit from the light actor.
So the mover functionality is gone from the inheritance hierarchy.
Instead, we're going to turn that into a component, and then we're going to place that component into
each one of these actors.
So it doesn't matter if your static mesh actor, light actor or whatever you are, you can just get
a mover component and move.
So that is how composition would really help out in this situation.
And now that we understand the differences between inheritance and composition, we're going to continue
in the next lecture and create a mover component that we're going to strap onto objects and make them
move.
I'll see you in the next lecture.
```

### Pointers 101 {#pointers-101}

```
Hello and welcome back.
In this lecture we're going to talk about pointers in C plus plus.
Let's get started.
So before we begin I should say that pointers are an advanced concept in C plus plus.
So if this is your very first time learning them they might not make sense to you which is completely
normal.
Just learn how to use them and carry on with the rest of the course and in the future.
Feel free to get back here and watch this again.
And one day it's just going to click.
Just give it some time.
With that being said, we're going to start by taking a look at variables and memory.
So each time you create a variable, it is stored inside of the memory of the computer, which is like
a giant block of ones and zeros.
The variable that you create is going to have an address.
And the address of the variable is simply the location of the variable within that memory, within that
giant block of ones and zeros of the computer.
We can get this address using the ampersand or the address of operator.
So let's say that we have a float called my float.
We can get the address of this variable by using the ampersand operator like this.
Place it before the name of the variable.
And then you're going to get the address.
The address of a variable is simply an integer number.
So for example it might look like this in decimal form.
You can also represent it in binary which we're going to do in just a second.
But in the end it's just an integer number showing you where this variable is in the giant memory of
the computer.
So here we have a visualization of the memory of the computer.
Like I said, just a giant array of ones and zeros.
And let's say that we're going to create an actor variable which is a class.
It is called My Actor.
Once we create this variable a piece of the memory is going to get allocated in this case represented
with the red color here.
And this is going to be the memory of this my actor variable.
When we use the ampersand operator to get the address of this variable, it's going to point to the
exact same location to the start of this block of memory that was allocated for my actor.
So that's what the address of a variable is.
It's literally the start of the memory that was allocated for that variable.
So why am I talking about variables and memory and addresses.
That is because pointer is simply a data type that can store an address a memory address to be specific.
And after we store it inside of a pointer, we can use that pointer to access that same memory, just
like we're using the variable itself.
In order to create a pointer, we're going to use the asterisk or the star operator.
When we're declaring a new variable, we're going to use it just after the data type.
So let's say that we have the My Float again right here.
In order to create a pointer that points to that memory location, we're going to type in float.
And we're going to place down a star.
This can be right next to the float data type.
Or it can be right next to the name of the variable.
Both ways is fine.
After that the name of the variable.
In this case it is pointer to my float.
And then we can assign the address of that my float variable into this pointer variable.
And since my float is a float, the pointer that points to it has to be a float as well.
You can't create an integer star and try to point it at my float.
Not going to work.
It has to be the same type.
Let's take a look at a visualization of this to make better sense of it.
Here we have the memory of the computer again.
We're going to create the My Actor variable again.
It's going to allocate a chunk of memory for it.
And we just saw that the address of my actor is going to point to that same location.
Now we're going to create a pointer an actor pointer variable called your actor.
And we're going to assign the address of my actor to this your actor pointer.
When we do that, the address of my actor will be stored in the memory of the computer, in this case
represented with the yellow right here.
And this is what the your actor pointer is storing.
It's literally the address of my actor.
And we know that the address of my actor is going to point to the memory location of my actor, which
means the pointer has the address that points to my actor's memory location, which means that we can
use the pointer to access that memory, which is the entire point of a pointer.
No pun intended.
Now let's take a look at how to access that memory through the pointer.
In order to do that, we're going to use the asterisk operator again, this time to dereference the
pointer and access the value it is pointing to.
So here's our old friend my float again.
And here is the pointer that points to it.
Now we can take this pointer and put an asterisk in front of it to access the value that is inside of
that memory, in this case 10.5.
So in this example we're creating a float called value.
And we're setting it to the dereferenced value of this pointer which is 10.5.
So this way we can get the value of my float and use it in an expression.
Question.
And we can also assign something to my float in the same way.
This time we're going to place the asterisk pointer to my float to the left side of the assignment.
And we're going to give it a different value.
And this piece of code right here will actually change the value of my float.
So even though we're not touching my float at all, since we're working with the same memory, we're
able to change it.
Which is why pointers are awesome.
So far, we saw a pointer to a float and a pointer to an actor, but there are no limits.
We can create pointers to any data type.
All the basic data types integer, float, structs, classes, everything can turn into a pointer variable.
You just have to place down an asterisk when you create the variable.
So for example here we have a vector called my vector.
And here is a pointer variable that is going to store an address of a vector variable, and we're assigning
the address of my vector right here.
Getting the address using the ampersand operator.
After we create this pointer, we can dereference it and access the xyz properties of my vector.
But since we have to use a dot operator with a struct, it's going to be a little bit more involved.
First we need to dereference it using the asterisk.
And then we need to place down a pair of parentheses and then use the dot operator to access, in this
case the x.
This is a little bit tedious.
And since this is such a popular thing to do in C plus plus, the creators of C Plus Plus created an
operator called the arrow operator, which does the same thing.
To create it, you need to place down a hyphen and a greater than sign, and it's going to do the same
exact thing here.
It's going to dereference it, and it's going to let you access the properties inside of that vector.
So this would be the proper way to do it.
And this way you can access the memory of my vector through this pointer.
Now let's talk about the advantages of using pointers.
Why use them at all in the first place?
Well they have a lot of uses.
But let's take a look at one example.
Let's say that we want to create a copy of a variable.
Here we have an integer a.
It's set to be ten.
And in order to create a copy you might go ahead and create another integer called b and then assign
the value of a into that new integer.
When you do this assignment, the original memory of A will be copied into B if you're using integers
or floats.
If you're using small data types, it's not going to be a problem because they're so small.
They're easy work for the CPU.
But this can become costly when you start dealing with larger data types like classes and structs,
for example, the actor class.
If you try to do a copy like this, it's going to copy everything that was inside of that actor.
Here, let's take a look at a visualization of this problem.
We have the My Actor variable.
And here is the memory that was allocated for my actor.
And now we're going to create a regular actor variable.
This is not a pointer.
As you can see it's called your actor.
And here is the memory that was allocated for your actor.
When we make this assignment assigning my actor into your actor, all of the memory, everything that
was inside of the red will be moved or copied into your actor.
So we essentially copied all of that memory into here.
And as this memory grows, as you have bigger and bigger classes or structs that you're working with,
this operation is going to become more and more costly.
Let's take a look at how we can fix this problem using a pointer instead of using a regular variable.
Here we have my actor.
Here we have your actor.
This time a pointer, and we're setting your actor to the address of my actor.
This time we only have a single large block of memory.
Storing the contents of my actor and your actor is simply a memory address.
Storing the address of my actor.
Which we know that this is going to let us access that memory.
So in this example, we're able to create the copy, access the same memory without having to duplicate
it.
And this is the power of a pointer.
So I think that's enough explanation for pointers, at least for now.
As time goes on we're going to learn more and more about them.
For this lecture we're going to leave it here.
And in the next one we're going to start actually using them inside of our code.
I'll see you in the next one.
```

### Pointer Practice {#pointer-practice}

That's why we have these letters like A and C.
These are also numbers.
It's just displayed in hexadecimal instead of decimal or binary.
Now this is great, but usually when we use pointers, at least here in unreal, it's going to be with
structs or classes because a float is a very small data type.
You really don't have to use a pointer to get some performance bonuses.
But as we learned in the previous lecture, with large structs and classes, passing them around by

That's why usually we have pointers to those large structs or classes to make it easier to have references
to them and have them passed around.
So let's create some space here with a comment, and let's start to have an example with an F vector.

### Owner Goes Back {#owner-goes-back}

```

### FVector Equals {#fvector-equals}

```
Welcome back to another lecture.
In this one, we're going to fix one of the flaws of our movement code and learn how to compare two
vectors to each other.
Let's go ahead and get started okay.
So at the moment inside of the tick component function, we are running this code in order to set the
location of the owning actor.
This is fine, but the problem is we're doing this even after we reach the target location.
After we reach the target location, we don't need to set the location anymore.
So here is what we need to do in order to fix this.
First of all, we're going to take a look at the current location, which we're getting right here.
And then we're going to compare that to the target location.
Is the current location equal to the target location?
If it is, that means we have reached the target location.
So we don't need to run this code anymore.
But if the current location is not equal to the target location.
That means we still have some distance to cover.
And in this case we want to run this code.
So first of all we're going to do this check.
And then we're going to write an if statement in order to control whether or not we should.
Execute this code right here.
So first of all let me show you how to check if a vector is equal to another vector.
We're going to use a function from the f vector struct.
This is a member function.
We're going to access it using the dot operator.
And this is called equals.
It is going to take in the vector that we want to check against.
In this case that is going to be the target location.
If you hover over this function you will see that it returns a boolean.
And the return value is going to be true if the vectors are equal within tolerance limits.
But that's not important.
The important part is that this is going to check the current location against the target location,
it's going to return true if they're equal and it's going to return false if they're not equal.
We're going to save this in a member variable that we're going to create.
This is going to be a new property visible anywhere.
We don't need to change it from the editor I just want to see it.
And we will call this reached target.
And by default it's going to be false.
Now we're going to assign reach target to the return value of the equals call.
This way we're going to be able to see whether or not we are at the target location from the editor
by taking a look at this visible anywhere variable.
Let's actually go ahead and compile this before we even implement if statement, just to see the value
of reach target in the game.
I'm going to exit out of the editor, do a rebuild from here because we created a header variable.
Start the project again and we're going to play the game.
Eject out of the player.
Find the mover.
You can see that the reach target boolean is set to be true because right now should move.
Boolean is false, which means the target location is the start location.
And the door happens to be exactly at the start location, which means we have reached the target.
So this is correct.
When I said should move to true reach, target is going to be false as you can see.
And once it reaches the other target location, the one with the move offset, it is going to be true
again.
And this is going to be the same as we go in the other direction while we're moving.
Reach target is false and once we reach the target it is true.
This means we can use this boolean to only run the movement code while we're moving to the target location.
In order to do that, we're going to write an if statement, and we're going to place all of the movement
code here into that if statement, just like that.
And the condition is going to be checking the reach target and making sure that it is false.
I'm going to give that to you as a challenge.
So go ahead and write the condition for that if statement.
You're going to check if reach target boolean is equal to false.
And if you forgot the equality operator, is this just a two equal signs back to back?
So pause the video, give it a go, and come back when you're done.
All right so inside of the parentheses we're going to type out reached target equals false.
This expression right here is going to be true when the reach target is false.
And it's going to be false when reach target is true which is exactly what we want.
So while we're going towards the target location, which target is false, which means the movement
code will be executed.
And once we reach the target location, reach target will be true and this expression will be false.
So we're going to stop making calls to the movement code.
In order to see this working better.
We can actually log something out here.
We'll say something like mover is moving and you will see that this will only be printed while the actor,
the owning actor is moving.
Let's run live coding with this.
Play the game, eject and get the mover as usual.
And now let's also pay attention to the output log as it is moving.
We're going to get those logs, as you can see right here.
But once it reaches the target location we are not getting any more logs.
I can clear it.
Right click clear log and you will see that they are not being printed anymore.
But as soon as I turn, the should move off and the target location is set back to the start location.
We're going to get those logs again, which means this code is now being executed.
And likewise, once it reaches the start location or the target location, which is now the start location,
we don't get the logs anymore.
Perfect.
Which means everything we did is working.
So that's going to be it.
For this lecture, we learned how to compare two vectors to each other using the equals function.
And now we're only executing the movement code while we are going towards the target location.
And once we do that, once we reach the target we stop which is going to be more efficient.
This is better code.
We have just improved it.
Great.
And finally, we don't need to log this.
This was just for test purposes.
So let's leave it here and continue in the next lecture.

### Logical Not Operator {#logical-not-operator}

```
Hello and welcome.
In this lecture we're going to learn about the logical not operator and simplify one of our if statements.
Let's go ahead and get started okay.
So here is the if statement that I want to simplify.
And to be honest with you what we did here is completely fine.
We're checking if the reach target boolean is equal to false.
The thing is with boolean values like reach target, we don't have to do these checks because reach
target being a boolean itself, has a true or false value.
So instead of doing a check like this, we can just go ahead and use that value instead.
This is exactly what we did up here which should move.
We're using should move the variable, the boolean itself as the condition.
And if it is true, we're executing this code.
And if it is false we're executing the code in the else.
So in this case it worked perfectly.
But in our case here it's not going to be that simple.
We can't just do this if we just have reach target inside of the condition.
This is going to be the opposite of what we're trying to achieve.
If reach target is true, we're going to execute the movement code.
And if it is false, we're going to ignore the movement code, which is just, like I said, the opposite
of what we're trying to achieve.
We need to execute the movement code.
If reach target is false and we need to ignore the movement code if reach target is true.
In order to do that, we need to somehow reverse the value of reach target.
And it turns out in C plus plus there is an operator to do just that.
That operator is called the logical not operator, and it is used with an exclamation mark.
This operator is going to reverse the result of the expression.
So if the expression is true, it's going to be false.
And if it is false, it's going to be true.
So here is how to use it.
Just like I said, it is an exclamation mark.
This is the logical not operator.
We're going to place it on the left hand side of an expression.
In this case we have the keyword true and not true is going to be false.
So that is how you read this.
Not true.
And just like I said that is going to be false.
Here we have the false keyword and not false is going to be true.
So just like I said, it is going to reverse whatever value we have here.
Now let's take a look at a little bit more realistic examples.
Here we have a check.
Is eight less than seven.
No that is false.
And we have a not in front of it.
So that false is going to be true.
Here we have ten is greater than nine which is true.
And we have a not in front of it.
So the result of that is going to be false.
Here we have a variable is alive.
It's a boolean.
It's set to be false.
And we have not is alive.
Which is going to be true because is alive is false and not false is going to be true.
So this is what the logical not operator is.
It is going to reverse the result.
And you're going to use it by placing down an exclamation mark on the left hand side of the expression
or the variable.
So now that we understand the logical not operator, we're going to use it in the if condition, in
order to make the if statement more concise and more simple.
And I'm going to give that to you as a challenge.
So go ahead and rewrite the if condition we had, which was which target is equal to false this time
using the logical not operator.
Pause the video, give it a go, and come back when you're done.
All right.
So I already did some of the work for you.
We got rid of the equality and the false, and now all we have to do is place down the logical not operator
in front of the target variable.
And this is going to create the effect that we want.
If reach target is false, meaning we haven't reached our target yet, the condition here will be true
and we will execute the movement code.
And if reach target is true, meaning we have reached the target location, the condition here is going
to be false and we're going to stop executing the movement code.
Let's go ahead and run live coding with this.
And then we're going to test the game.
It should be working the same way.
There should be no difference.
But this time we are going to be using the brand new and awesome logical, not operator so we can eject,
click on the mover or find mover rather, and then we can just turn it on and off using the should move
boolean as usual.
And you can see that once it reaches the target, it's going to stop.
And it's going to be the same on the other way as well.
So it is working exactly like it was.
But now we're using the very cool, logical not operator.
This is the usual way I would write this condition.
I never write it like this.
So this is what we had before.
When I'm writing code by myself, I never do this.
Although this is fine.
If you did this, I mean it would be fine.
It's easier to read in my opinion, but this is the usual way cplusplus developers write code.
It is a little bit more difficult for beginners to read this.
That's why we did this one first.
But you're going to see this far more often than you see this.
Okay, so that is the logical not operator.
We're going to leave it here for this lecture and continue in the next one.
I'll see you there.

### C++ Compilation Steps {#c-compilation-steps}

```
Hello and welcome.
In this lecture, we're going to take a deeper look at the compilation process of a Cplusplus Unreal
project.
Let's get started.
So far in the course we have worked inside of a single class.
We had a header file and a cpp file.
Very soon we're going to create multiple classes, and already we had to include another header file
so that we get access to the v interp constant to function.
I wanted to create this lecture to shed some light into the compilation process, so that we can better
understand how all of these files come together in order to create a single executable.
So let's take a look at this explanation of the different steps of the compilation.
First of all, we have our source code.
This is all of the cplusplus and files we have inside of the Visual Studio project.
The first step is going to be feeding all of these files into a tool called the Unreal Header Tool.
This is a program from the Unreal Engine, and it's going to take a look at all of our source code files
for unreal specific syntax, and it's going to generate more cplusplus code.
We can take a look at the header file, and you will see that we have these unreal specific sections
like the Uclass.
This dungeon scape API keyword, the generated body, the view properties.
All of these are special unreal syntax.
That unreal header tool is going to take a look at, and it's going to generate more code in order to
create the connection between the C plus plus code and Unreal Engine.
We don't need to understand the specifics of how the header tool works, we just need to be aware that
it is there after the unreal header tool.
We're going to go into the standard C plus plus compilation.
The header tool section is specific to unreal, but these steps, the standard steps are the same for
every Cplusplus project.
The first step is the preprocessor.
After that we have the compiler and finally the linker.
The preprocessor is somewhat similar to the unreal header tool.
It's going to take a look at all of the source code files.
And it's going to look for preprocessor directives.
These are the lines of code that start with the hash symbol.
These are special directives to the preprocessor in order to do certain things.
For example the include preprocessor directive right here is going to copy the contents of this file
into here.
And we have many other directives.
And the preprocessor is going to take a look at all of the files and generate more code and do certain
things to the code files.
So far at this point we haven't compiled anything.
That is the next step.
We're going to give all of those files processed by the preprocessor into the compiler, and the compiler
is going to compile all of those files into binary executable code, but these are still going to be
multiple separate files.
And the final step is going to be feeding all of those executable binary code files into the linker.
And the linker is going to create all of the necessary connections and spit out a final a single executable.
So these are the overall steps of the compilation of an unreal Cplusplus project.
Now let's take a look at an example.
And we're going to focus on the standard Cplusplus compilation steps.
So here we have three different code files game dot, CP, math.h, and cp.
In Math.h we have the declaration for a function called add, and the definition for this function is
inside of cp.
It's a very simple function that takes in two integers, adds them together, and returns them in CP.
We are including the header file math.h and inside of a function called mygame, we're making a call
to add.
We have to include the header file here.
Otherwise we don't know what the add function is.
This is how we create that connection between these files.
But still the definition is in the cpp file.
And we haven't included that here.
So let's see how this is going to unfold.
The first step the preprocessor is going to go through all of these.
And it's going to create these dot I intermediate files.
In the case of cpp the preprocessor is going to get rid of the include line here.
And it's going to change that with the contents of the header file.
So everything that we had in Math.h is now inside of this game dot I intermediate file.
After that the preprocessor is going to process math dot cpp.
But since it doesn't have any preprocessor directives, it's going to be exactly the same.
The only difference is the dot ai file extension.
Then we're going to give these files into the compiler, and the compiler is going to compile them into
these dot obj files.
This is that binary machine code that the computer can understand.
So now the C plus plus human readable code is gone.
And we're working with machine code or binary code.
So even though we compiled these into machine readable code they are still separate files.
So we're going to give these into the linker.
And the linker is going to link them into a single executable.
This step is crucial.
And it's going to fix the problem that I mentioned a second ago.
The definition for add is inside of this file and we don't have access to that in game dot cpp.
We included the header but we don't have the definition.
This is the step that connection is created.
The definition for add is inside of this obj file.
And we need to access that from the obj file.
That connection is created by the linker and everything is placed into a single file called game.exe.
So hopefully this whole process is now a little bit more clear for you.
And now it's time for your challenge.
And the challenge is why do we include mover dot h.
This should be kind of obvious from the explanation, but nonetheless I want you to comment out the
include mover line from the mover cpp file.
It's at the top.
And then I want you to try to recompile and take a look at the errors that you get.
Pause the video, give it a go and come back when you're done.
All right we'll go into mover dot CPP.
And we're going to comment out this line.
So we'll just place two slashes at the start.
Save this file.
And then we can run live coding.
Let's take a look at all the errors we get.
And we get a bunch of them here.
If we go to the top you will see that the first one is you.
Mover is not a class missing type specifier.
Undeclared identifier.
Not a class.
Undeclared identifier not found.
So basically the problem is in the cpp file we don't know what the class is.
It is not defined or declared anywhere inside of this file.
And the compiler is confused.
It's telling us you're trying to do something with this class, but I don't even know what it is.
You haven't declared it.
Where is this class?
It's not declared.
So that is why we have to include the header file in the cpp file, so that when we try to compile it,
the compiler actually knows what we're talking about.
And that is exactly why we had to include this one, so that we can access the F math class without
the include without the header file.
The compiler has no idea what this is, and that is a brief look into the compilation process of a C
plus Plus Unreal project.

### Logical And Operator {#logical-and-operator}

```
Hello and welcome.
In this lecture, we're going to learn how to use the logical and operator in C plus plus.
Let's go ahead and get started.
So in the previous lecture we made a call to actor hashtag on the other actor variable which is an actor
pointer right here.
We made a dire mistake.
Can you spot the mistake that we made?
Well, the mistake is using a pointer without checking if it is valid or not.
So instead of using it immediately, we have to do an if statement.
Check with the other actor.
And only after we confirm that it is a valid pointer we can use it.
So we're going to put everything into this if statement.
We have to do the same thing down here as well.
So right here we will do the same check.
And you might be thinking that we are getting this actor pointer as an argument.
But even then there is a chance that it is null pointer.
So we still have to do this check.
With this new addition, we have three if statements right here nested in each other.
Now this is completely fine.
There's nothing wrong with this, but there is a shorter way of writing the same logic.
In order to do that, we need to learn the logical and operator.
So let's take a look at that.
The logical and operator is a double ampersand just like this.
It is going to return true if the left and right hand side values that you provide are true.
Just like this, we have the logical and operator on the left side we have true.
On the right side we have true.
And this whole expression is going to evaluate to true.
If you have a false value in this case the right side, it's going to evaluate to false.
If the false is on the left side it's still going to be false.
And if you have false in left and right.
It's also going to be false.
So with this operator, both the left and right side has to be true.
If you want to get a true evaluation in any other case, if there's a false in the expression, it is
going to be false.
Let's take a look at a couple of examples.
Here we have two variables one boolean called hashtag which is assigned true.
Then we have an actor pointer which is assigned to a valid address of a variable.
So this will also be true if we use the and operator with these.
Since left and right are true, this will also evaluate to true.
Here we have a true expression on the left negative ten less than five.
And then a false expression on the right five equals two.
So this whole and operator is going to be false.
Here we have five equals to again which is false.
And then on the right side we have my actor which is true.
This is also going to be false.
And finally we have five equals to again which is false.
And then not has stag which is also false.
So this whole thing is going to be false as well.
So these are some examples of using the logical and operator.
One interesting attribute of the and operator is that we can chain them together.
So let's say that we have these variables here.
Again we can write one big chained statement like this.
So we have hashtag and my actor and negative ten less than five and five equals two.
When the compiler is evaluating this first of all it's going to take a look at the leftmost expression.
I have placed purple parentheses over this so that it is obvious that this will be the first one that
is evaluated.
In this case, this is going to be true, and then the next one is going to be the negative ten less
than five.
So we will take the true on the left and then do the negative ten less than five which is also true.
So that is also going to evaluate to true.
And then finally we will evaluate the end with the five equals two which in this case is false.
So the final result is going to be false as well.
So this is how to chain multiple logical and operations together.
And you can also see that if there is a false in the equation if anything is false, the final result
is definitely going to be false with the and operator.
Another attribute of the logical and is short circuiting.
If the left hand side expression evaluates to false, the right hand side will not be evaluated.
So it's going to short circuit.
It's going to return early from the expression.
In this case we have five equals to on the left.
The compiler is going to take a look at the first expression which is on the left.
And it's essentially going to stop right there because the result is going to be false anyways.
There's no need to evaluate what is on the right side.
The left side is false And with the logical and operator, we know that if one of the sides are false,
it is 100% is going to be false.
So the compiler is not even going to do the evaluation here, because it would be just a waste of computation
power.
Turns out this attribute of the logical and operator makes it very useful when checking pointers.
Let's say that we have an actor pointer called my actor and it is currently set to be null pointer.
Previously we would do a check like this.
First of all, we would check if my actor is valid and then inside of that if statement we would try
to use it.
In this case, by making a call to actor has tag.
Using the logical and operator.
We can combine these two if conditions together, and we can do that essentially because of the short
circuit property.
So here we have a logical and operator.
On the left hand side we have my actor which is null pointer.
And on the right hand side we're making a call to actor has tag, which we know is going to return a
boolean.
But in this case, because my actor is null pointer, trying to make a call to actor has tag on a null
pointer would crash the game, but since we're first checking if my actor is valid or not on the left
hand side, the right hand side is only going to get evaluated if the left hand side is true.
So in this case, my actor is false.
So the logical and operation here will be short circuited and the right hand side won't be evaluated.
But if the my actor pointer was valid, then we would also check if we had the tag because we need to
know both sides in that case to determine if it's going to be true or false.
So this is how to do a pointer check using the logical and operator.
Now I'm going to give you a challenge with your newfound knowledge of the logical and operator.
I want you to go ahead and simplify the if statements into a single one using the logical and operator.
I'm talking about these two if statements right here.
So combine these ones into a single condition using the logical and operator.
You don't have to do this one because we're going to take this out to a different function in a later
lecture.
So you can leave this, but combine these two into a single one.
And for bonus points you can also do it here as well.
It's going to be the same operation.
So pause the video, give it a go and come back when you're done.
All right let's go ahead.
And here place down a logical and operator.
And then we can essentially just copy and paste what is here.
And then we can essentially get rid of this if statement.
So I will take what's inside of it, paste it into this if statement.
And then I will delete this one.
Just like that down here we are going to do the same thing.
So I will take this part, put a logical and operator, cut this part and then get rid of this part.
So now functionally the code is the same but we are doing the check in a single if statement.
First of all we will take a look at the other actor pointer.
Is it valid.
And if only it is valid we will do the right hand side.
If for some reason this pointer is invalid, we're going to short circuit and the if statement is going
to fail without making a call to actor hashtag.
Great.
So we can go ahead and run live coding with this.
Everything is going to be the same gameplay wise, but just to check that it is indeed working, we
can run the game.
Why not?
Great.
So that is how to use the logical and operator in order to take two if statement conditions and turn
it into a single one.
We're going to leave it here for this lecture and continue in the next one.

### Public & Private {#public-private}

```
Welcome back.
In this lecture we're going to talk about the public and private access modifiers in C plus plus.
Let's go ahead and get started.
So in C plus plus we have these public, private and protected keywords.
Collectively these are called the access modifiers.
They're called the access modifiers because they're used to assign accessibility levels to class members.
When I say class members I mean member variables and member functions of a class.
Let's take a look at how these keywords are used.
Inside of a class.
You're going to type out the name of the modifier that you want to use, in this case public.
Then you're going to place down a colon.
And after that, every variable and function you declare is going to have that level of accessibility.
In this case should move would be a public variable.
After that, you can use another access modifier to create another block.
In this case everything after this private declaration would be private, and you can use these as many
times as you want.
In this lecture, we're going to take a look at the public and private access modifiers.
And we're going to do protected in a later lecture.
So first of all, let's take a look at public.
If a class member is declared as public it is going to be available everywhere.
Functions outside of the class are able to access it and change it.
On the other hand, we have private and these members cannot be accessed from outside.
They're only available within the member functions of the class.
So these names are quite straightforward.
If something is public, it is public.
It can be accessed from the outside.
If something is private, it is private.
It cannot be accessed from the outside.
It is private to the class.
Let's take a look at some examples.
First of all, we have a public example here.
We have the U class and it has the should move boolean in a public block we can access the should move
boolean inside of the Beginplay function of you mover.
This would work.
We knew this.
We can create a variable and use it inside of one of the member functions.
This is not news.
We knew this.
This will work.
But here is the important part.
Here we have the trigger function from the trigger components.
And inside of that we have a pointer to a mover component.
We're accessing the should move variable using the arrow operator.
And we're setting it to true.
This is going to work because the should move Boolean is set to be public.
We're accessing it outside of the mover class in a different class.
In this case the trigger component.
And like I said this is going to work.
We are actually doing this right now inside of our code.
And it is working because of the public accessibility of should move.
Some people might have a problem with this because in object oriented programming, you're not supposed
to access member variables from outside of the class like this.
So some people consider this bad practice.
Now let's take a look at a private example.
In this case we have the you mover class.
Again with the should move boolean.
But this time it is inside of a private block.
So this variable has private accessibility.
We're assigning true to it inside of Beginplay.
And this is still going to work.
Even if a member variable is private we can still access it inside of our own member functions.
Beginplay is a function of the mover class.
So we can access the private variable no problem.
But here is the different part.
Inside of the trigger component we're not able to access should move anymore, because should move is
a private variable, and you can't access it and assign to it from outside of it.
In the world of object oriented programming, this would be a better practice making the member variable
private and not letting outside classes to access that variable.
This is also called data hiding.
You can also hear the term encapsulation.
These just mean hiding the data of the class as private and not letting anyone else access it, just
like I said.
But you might be thinking, if this is private, how am I supposed to set it from the trigger?
Following the same object oriented approach, there is a concept called setter and getter functions.
Let's take a look at these.
Just like the previous example, we're going to create the should move boolean in a private block and
inside of a public block.
We're going to create two functions to access and modify the private should move variable.
Get should move is going to return a boolean.
This is going to be should move and set.
Should move is going to take in a boolean and it's going to assign it to should move.
With this approach no one is going to access should move directly If they want to get the value, they're
going to call this public function.
And if they want to assign something to should move, they're going to call this public function.
Now in this example inside of the trigger function we're calling set should move with the true keyword.
Instead of setting should move directly.
And when we want to get the value of should move we're making a call to get should move according to
object oriented principles.
This would be the right way to do it, but that is an opinion.
Not everyone thinks like that, but this is something that you should definitely know how to do.
Finally, we're going to take a look at the default access modifier, which is private.
So if you create a class and you don't specify anything, the variables and functions you create are
going to be private by default.
Great.
So that is enough information about public and private.
Now let's do a challenge.
I want you to take the should move boolean in the movers header and place it in a private block.
Then I want you to create public getter and setter functions for should move.
The getter is going to be called get should move.
It is going to return a boolean.
It's not going to take in any arguments.
And inside of the function body you're going to return the should move variable.
That's it.
The setter is going to be called set should move.
This is a naming convention by the way.
When you're creating getter and setter functions you would say get variable name set variable name.
That way anyone that looks into your code will understand that these are getter and setter functions.
The setter is going to return nothing so it will be void.
It is going to take in a single argument.
