### Keeping Count {#keeping-count}

```
Hello and welcome back.
In this lecture, we're going to keep track of how many actors we have on top of the pressure plate,
and we're only going to deactivate it once all of those actors are out.
Let's go ahead and get started okay.
So at the moment we have a decent pressure plate functionality in the game.
But there is one problem with this.
When we have multiple actors on top of the pressure plate and one of them exits, it is going to deactivate
the mover even though the player is still standing on top of it.
So what we want to do is we want to only deactivate the mover once the final actor gets off.
In order to do that, we're going to create an integer called activator count for the trigger component.
At the start, this is going to be zero, and we're going to increase or decrease this based on the
actors on the pressure plate.
Once an actor comes on, we're going to increase it by one, let's say in this case, three actors come
on and each time we're going to increase activator count by one just like this, we'll go up to three.
Once an actor gets off, we're going to decrease it.
So in this case we would go down to two.
Let's say another got off.
We would go down to one.
And finally the last actor gets off and we will go down to zero.
And this is the point.
We would deactivate the mover while we have actors on the pressure plate.
While the activator count is greater than zero.
Even if an actor gets off, we're not going to deactivate the mover.
That is the plan.
Let's go ahead and start working on this.
First of all, inside of the trigger component we're going to create the activator count variable.
It is going to be a visible anywhere so that we can see the value while we're playing the game.
It is going to be an integer called activator count.
And we will give it zero as the default value.
Let me actually show you a different type of integer data type called an integer.
32.
This is what is recommended by unreal.
It is exactly like a regular integer, but this is going to have a 32 bit size guaranteed, which is
good when you're making games.
You want to know how big the data type is going to be, and it is good that it is guaranteed 32 bits.
You can see that in the overlap begin and on overlap end functions.
The code we got from the documentation is also using int 32.
Functionally it's going to be the same.
There is nothing different for using them, but just know that this type of integer is recommended by
unreal.
Okay, back to the topic.
We have the activator count and at the start it is zero.

and decrease it when something stops overlapping.

After doing the necessary checks, we will say activator count equals activator count minus one.

Right here.

one.

Creators of C plus Plus created these shorthand operators.

### Collision 101 {#collision-101}

```
Hello and welcome back.
In this lecture we're going to do an introduction to the collision system in unreal.
Let's go ahead and get started.
So what I want to do is I want to turn this key item, mesh the glass statue into a mesh that doesn't
collide.
I want to be able to just jump and go straight through it.
In order to do that, first we need to understand how the collision system works a little bit better.
So let's go ahead and create ourselves a little test area over here.
I'm going to duplicate a chair and a table.
And we're going to use these as our test subjects.
Great.
So what is a collision in the first place.
What does it mean.
Well a collision occurs when two actors touch each other just like that in unreal.
There is a whole system that determines how are these actors going to respond to each other?
Are they going to stop?
Are they going to overlap.
Are they going to ignore each other?
So let's learn how this system works.
First of all, we're going to click on the chair and click on the static mesh component, which is the
component that is going to actually do the collision.
We're going to scroll down in the details pane and go into the collision section which is right here.
In here we have a bunch of different settings.
But the most important currently is going to be the collision presets.
And currently this is set to default.
If you click on it you can see a bunch of different presets that we can pick.
These are essentially pre-made collision settings for the most common cases created by unreal.
For example, we have a pawn collision preset for actors controlled by the player.
We have a physics actor collision preset for actors that simulate physics.
These are great.
We can.
Most of the time just use one of these, but we can also create a custom one ourselves.
So let's click on that.
Once you do that, the collision preset is going to be visible, and you can see that we have all of
these different options here that we can play with.
Let's go through them one by one and try to understand what they do.
First of all we have collision enabled.
This determines what type of collision this actor can do.
We essentially have four different options.
The first one is no collision, which means exactly what it sounds like.
The actor has zero collision.
It is not represented in the physics engine.
If you choose this like that and play the game, you are going to go straight through that chair because
it doesn't have any collision anymore, just like that.
After that, we have an option called query only, which means this actor can do things like overlaps,
it can do raycasts and sweeps, which we're going to see later in the section.
But it can't do physics things like jumping off of other actors.
It cannot really do, but it can do the overlaps and things like that.
After that, we have physics only, which means it can do the physics part, but this time it can't
do the query, so it can't do the overlaps and sweeps and things like that.
And finally we have collision enabled, which is what we had by default.
And this means it can do both query and physics.
And the final two here we are not concerned with at the moment.
So that is what collision enabled is.
And after that we have the object type.
This is how this object is represented in the physics engine.
It is the type of that object.
You can see that the chair is set to be a world static type.
This is an actor that doesn't move.
And static mesh actors are set to be world static for the most part.
If you click on this list, you will see the default object types that we get from unreal.
We can also create one ourselves if we choose to do so.
We have a type called World Dynamic, which is an actor that moves with code.
So for example, the door that we have here, this is moved with the mover component.
So this would be a world dynamic.
We can click on the static mesh component and take a look at the preset, and you can see that the type
is set to be, well, dynamic.
So that's a good example of that.
Let's go back into our chair.
The next type we have here is called pawn.
And this is for player controlled actors.
We have a physics body type for actors that have physics simulation.
We have a vehicle type destructible type.
You get the idea.
We have these different types for different objects inside of our game.
So why is this object type important?
It is important because it is used down here to determine how we're going to respond to collisions with
other types.
So down here in the collision responses section we have ignore overlap and block the three different
types of collisions we can have in unreal.
Ignore means that two actors that collide are basically going to go through each other without any collisions.
That is ignore.
Then we have overlap, which means the actors are still going to go through each other, but we're going
to generate overlap events that we can use inside of our code.
This is what we did with the trigger component.
But besides, those events overlap and ignore look exactly the same within the game.
Two actors moving through each other.
And then finally we have block, which means two actors are going to block each other.
They're going to stop when they collide.
And inside of this collision responses section, we determine which one of these are going to be done
when we collide with an object type.
So in this case with this preset we are set to block every other type.
You can see the types that we just saw world, static world, dynamic pawn, physics, body.
Everything is in here.
We also have this trace responses section.
But we're going to take a look at this later.
For now we're going to focus on these object types.
In this preset we're set to block each one of these.
So inside of this game it is expected that when we interact with the chair that it is going to be blocking.
As you can see I cannot go through it.
but using a custom collision preset, we can change those rules.
So for example, with the chair, I could make it overlap with the pawn if I wanted to.
Just like that.
And now when I play, I'm going to be able to overlap with it instead of blocking.
So that is the basic idea behind that section.
We are determining how this object behaves with collisions against other types.

### Input System in C++ {#input-system-in-c}

Basically it is going to be a two step process.
First of all, we're going to register the mapping context.
That is going to be the first part.
And after that we're going to connect each of our input actions to a C plus plus function.
When an action is triggered the connected C plus plus function will get called so that we can do something
about it.
For example, when the input action jump is triggered, a jump function is going to get called making
the player actually jump.
These input actions by themselves Cells don't do anything.
We have to connect them to the C plus plus functions so that they actually execute some code.

There's one important thing about these connections and that is this trigger event type.
This is going to determine at which point the function gets called.
Right now there are three different options that we need to understand started completed and triggered.
Started means that the input action is just started.
So if we're using a key it is just being pressed.
If we're using a mouse button it just got pressed.
If you use this type of trigger event the function is going to get called only once at the start.
Next up we have completed and this is when the input action is over.
When you take your hand off of the key, or when you take your finger off of the mouse button.
If you use this one, this is also going to make the function get called once when the input action
is completed.
And finally we have triggered, which means the key or the mouse button or the gamepad button is currently
being pressed down.
It is being triggered.
If you use this type of trigger event, the function that you connect will get called continuously until
you stop triggering the input action, and you can see that the move action is connected using the triggered
trigger event for something that needs to happen once, like jumping, we can use the started action,
but for something that needs to happen all the time, like moving, we need to use the triggered events.
So moving for example, as long as we have the keys held down, we want to continue moving, but for
jumping when we press the spacebar, for example, we only want to jump once.
So that is how the connection between the input action and a function is created.

### Line and Shape Tracing {#line-and-shape-tracing}

The next step is going to be detecting the lock actor and the collectible item actor when we press the
left mouse button.
So inside of the interact function we want to get access to these actors if we are close enough, and
if we are looking at them when we press the left mouse button, how can we do that?
We're going to use a technique called line tracing, also known as ray casting.
Let me explain how this works.
First of all, we're going to pick a starting location.
This is going to be a point in the game world.
It's going to be a vector.
Let's call this start.
This is going to be the location of the player.
And it's actually going to be the location of the camera component, because that is exactly where we're
looking from, then we're going to pick an end location.
This is also going to be a vector.
A point inside of our game.
We're going to calculate this using the start location.
Let's call this the end.
Then we're going to call a special function that is going to do a line trace.
That function is going to take in these points.
And it's going to trace a line from the start to the end.
This function is going to intersect with other actors.
And it's going to return the first actor it touches.
In this case we want that to be the collectible item or the lock actor.
This approach works fine, but there are two small problems with it.
The first one is that align trace can be very easy to miss because it is just a line.
It doesn't have any thickness.
So if you're looking at the item at a slightly bad angle, you're going to miss the endpoint and the
whole line trace is going to miss the collectible item or the lock.
We don't want this to happen.
And the second problem is other actors on the way.
So for example, in this case the line trace might hit the chair before it hits the collectible item.
And because it is going to give us the first actor it hits, it's going to be wrong.
So let's talk about how we're going to fix these problems.
For the first problem, instead of doing a line trace, we're going to do something very similar.
A shape trace.
Instead of tracing a line, we're going to trace a collision shape.
For the shape we're going to use a sphere.
So we're going to create a sphere.
And we're going to trace this just like we traced the line.
This is also called a sweep.
And this is going to give us a much bigger trace area.
So it's going to be much easier to hit the collectible item or the lock.
So that is how we're going to fix the first problem.
And for the second problem we're going to use a custom trace channel.
When you do align line trace or a shape trace, you can specify a trace channel, and then the trace
is only going to look for actors that block that trace channel.
In one of the previous lectures, we learned how the collision system works with the objects.
This is very similar.
So let's take a look at the collectible item here.
Go into the static mesh component.
Scroll down into the collision.
Previously we learned how to use these object responses with the static world dynamic and things like
that.
Now we're going to take a look at the trace responses which have the same ignore overlap and block.
And these are used for line traces shape traces things like that.
By default we have visibility and camera channels.
And we're going to create our own channel here, which is going to be ignored by default by every actor
for the collectible item and for the lock actor.
We're going to set this to block so that when we do a trace, we're going to give it this trace channel
and everything else will be ignored, and the trace is only going to look for collectible items and
the lock.
So that is how we're going to fix the second problem.
In this lecture we're going to go ahead and create that custom trace channel that's going to be inside
of the project settings.
So let's go there.
We're going to scroll down and inside of the engine section here we're going to go into collision.
In here we can create a custom trace channel right here.
We can also create a custom object channel.
If we wanted to this would be creating a new object type.
In this case we want to create a new trace channel.
So let's click on that.
We're going to give it a name interact.
And then the default response is going to be ignore.
Everything is going to ignore this channel.
And we're going to set the ones that we want to block manually.
Once you do that, you can go back into the test level and you need to click away from the glass statue,
click again, go into the collision preset And now you can see the new trace channel right here.
And you can see that by default it is set to be ignored.
We're going to go into the blueprints and set this to be block.
And that is going to be your challenge.
So go ahead and set the Interact Trace channel to block for the lock actor.
And you're going to do this for the trigger component and for the glass statue.
You're going to do this for the static mesh component.
Pause the video, give it a go and come back when you're done.
All right.
So first of all, we will go into my stuff into the test door lock.
And we will go into the trigger component because that is what I want to block for the channel.
We can also use the static mesh.
But that way you could also click on the table and click on the key item mesh and activate it.
I don't want that.
I only want the trigger component to be the place we click on.
So let's scroll down into the collision section.
We're going to turn the preset into custom.
And then we're going to set the interact to be blocked just like that.
We'll save this compile.
Go back this time.
Open up the blueprint glass statue.
And for this one we only have a static mesh component.
So we're going to have to use that one.
We'll go down make this a custom and then block the interact again just like that.
Now when we do a shape trace we're going to specify the interact trace channel.
And the trace is only going to look for actors that block that channel.
And since everything else is ignoring it by default, they're not going to be included.
For example, we could click on this chair static mesh component, take a look at the collision settings.
And if we set this to be custom you will see that interact is ignored.
Great.
So we're going to leave it here for this lecture and continue in the next one.

### C++ References {#c-references}

```
Hello and welcome.
In this lecture we're going to learn about references in C plus plus.
Let's go ahead and get started.
If you take a look at the sweep single by channel function signature, you will see that many of the
arguments have an ampersand next to the data type.
This is called a reference.
It is very similar to a pointer, but with some differences.
Basically, a reference is an alias for an existing variable.
It's just an alternative name to refer to the same variable.
In order to create one, you're going to use the ampersand after the data type.
Then you're going to give it a name, and then you're going to assign it to the variable that you want
to refer to.
In this case, float ref is a float reference and it is referring to my float which is declared up here.
After you create this connection, float ref is the same thing as my float.
Literally you can use the name float ref as if it is my float.
That is essentially what a reference is.
From that description, you can tell that the reference is very similar to a pointer, and it is true
it is essentially the same thing, the same purpose, but it is easier to use and it is less powerful.
Pointers have more functionality to them, but they are a little bit more difficult to use with all
the operators.
They can also be dangerous if you try to.
For example, dereference a null pointer, you're going to crash.
On the other hand, references are safer, they're easier to use, but they are more limited.
They're less powerful.
So here is how to use a reference.
We have the same setup here with the float ref and my float.
In order to access the value of my float using the reference, we can just use the float ref as it is
because just like I said, as far as the compiler is concerned when you mention float ref as if you're
mentioning my float.
So in this case we're taking the value which is 3.4, adding ten to it and then assigning that to a
new number float.
So the new number would be 13.4.
In this case here is how to do an assignment to my float using the reference.
And just like I said, all we have to do is use the float ref name, do the assignment and that's it.
This is what I was talking about when I said it is easier to use references compared to pointers.
With pointers, we would have to use some dereferencing operators here and here to make this work.
And also when we create the pointer, we would have to use the ampersand operator to get the address
of my float.
But with references.
As you can see, it is much simpler.
In this case, my float right here would be assigned to 4.5.
Here is how to create a reference with a struct.
And this would also be the same with a the class.
Here we're creating a Mivec effector and then we're creating an effector reference called Mivec ref.
After we do this, we can access X, Y, and z or the other properties of mivec with the reference using
the dot operator.
So unlike pointers, we don't have to use the arrow operator with the reference, we can just use the
regular dot operator.
We can also access the member functions using the dot operator as usual.
Here are some important rules with references.
First of all, once you create a reference, you can never reassign it to a different variable.
Here we're creating float ref and we're assigning my float to it.
After we do this, float ref can never refer to another variable.
This connection is for life.
So if I created another float right here and I tried to do something like this, this wouldn't create
a new reference.
This would just be taking whatever is inside of my other float and then assigning it into my float.
In this case, we would assign my float to 32.3.
Another rule is that a reference cannot be declared without a value.
So this is not allowed.
When you create a reference you have to provide a variable, otherwise it's not going to compile.
The next rule is that a reference can't be null pointer.
So you cannot do this.
This is also not allowed.
Keep these rules in mind when you use references.
And that's it.
That is what a reference is.
It is essentially just an alternative name to the same variable.
You don't have to use the arrow operator.
You don't have to use any dereferencing operators.
You can just create it and use it as if it is that original variable.
And now that you understand what a reference is, I want you to go ahead and create a local effector
and a reference to it.
You can do this inside of the interact function.
Then I want you to change the x, y and z using the reference.
After you do that, go ahead and log the effector before and after you make these changes.
You can use the two compact string function inside of the log to make it easier.
And of course compile and test.
For bonus points, you can log both the reference and the original variable before and after to see
if they're the same.
Pause the video, give it a go, and come back when you're done.
All right, let's go into Visual Studio.
Inside of the interact function.
We're going to create a little test scenario here.
So first of all we're going to create an effector called Mivec.
We'll set this to one two and three just like that.
And then we'll create the reference which is going to be a vector.
Ampersand to make it a reference.
And we'll just call this vec ref.
We're going to set it equal to my vec.
And now vec ref is essentially the same thing as mivec just under a different name.

### Pass By Reference {#pass-by-reference}

```
Hello and welcome.
In this lecture we're going to learn about reference arguments in functions in C plus plus.
Let's go ahead and get started okay.
So now that we understand what our references let's go ahead and understand the details of a reference
argument into a function.
First of all though, we need to understand how a regular argument is passed into a function.
This is called passing by value.
Basically, when we pass an argument or a variable into a function, a copy of that variable is going
to get created and the original variable will not be changed.
For example, here we have a function called test function.
It's taking in a float called my float, and it is setting my float to 10.3.
Here we're making a call to test function with this local variable called float, which is set to 3.4
when we make this function call right here, my float is going to get passed in to test function, and
a copy of my float is going to create it as the argument.
In this case it is also called my float because this is a copy of the original.
My float that we passed in is not going to change.
So after we make this function call right here, my float is still going to be 3.4.
Just like I explained, if the argument is modified in the function, the original will not change because
this one is simply a copy.
Normally, when we do want to return a value from a function, we're just going to use a return value.
We're going to set the return type.
In this case it's going to be float.
And then we're going to use a return statement to return the value.
And then when we use the function we're just going to assign the return value into my float in order
to use it.
This would be the normal way we do this, but turns out using references and also pointers.
There is another way of returning values from a function.
When an argument is a reference, also a pointer.
The change is made to.
It will actually change the original variable.
Here we have the test function again, this time taking in a float reference called my float, and again
it is setting that argument to 10.3.
This time when we make a call to it using my float local variable right here, my float is actually
going to get changed to 10.3 right here.
After we make the function call, because this time we are passing a reference.
This is the actual memory address of my float, and the changes we make inside of the function will
change the original.
This is because we are passing the memory address of the original variable into the function.
It's the reference.
It's not just a mere copy.
Here is an example of the same concept, this time with a pointer.
We have the test function taking in a float pointer called my float, and in the function body it is
dereferencing it and assigning 10.3.
And when we make the function call we're going to pass my float.
But this time we have to use the address of operator in order to pass the memory address.
This time as well.
My float variable that we created up here will actually get modified within the test function, and
after the function call, it is going to be 10.3.
Whether or not you use a reference or a pointer, this concept is called passing by reference.
This means we can use a reference argument as an additional return value.
So for example, we could do something like this a function called setup vector which is going to take
in an f vector reference called my vector.
And it's going to set x, y and z to ten, 20 and 30.
It's essentially setting the values of my vector.
And then we can use this function in order to set up any vector that we want.
For example, here I'm creating a my vector, passing it into setup vector and setup vector is going
to set the values for me.
You might be asking why do it this way?
Why not just create a return value and then return the vector, and then we can assign it back into
my vector?
That is a good idea.
That's completely fine.
But the thing is, with these types of arguments, we're not limited to a single return value.
We can have as many return values as we want.
What is done usually is that the return value will be a boolean, and if the boolean is true, that
means the function was successful, and if it is false, that means the function was unsuccessful and
then the actual return value will be an argument instead.
Just like this one.
This is just an example.
We're returning a boolean and then the actual return value is this argument right here.
Another advantage of using a reference or pointer argument is performance.
This way, we only need to pass in the memory address of the variable into the function, whereas if
you had an actual regular argument, you would have to copy everything into here and then return it
back into the function call.
So this way it is also more performance.
I should also mention that if an argument is a return value, it is usually going to have out in front
of its name.
So this would be out my vector for example.
Finally we're going to talk about the const keyword.
When you use this in an argument, that argument will not be able to be modified inside of the function.
So here as an example, we have a function that takes in a const factor reference called vector.
And because it is const I'm not going to be able to change it within the function body.
So this code would actually not compile.
It would throw an error.
You might be asking, if we can't change the argument, why would we pass it in as a reference?
And the answer again is performance.
Instead of passing the struct by value and copying everything into the function, we're simply passing
the reference.
We're simply passing the memory address so that we don't have to copy all of that stuff, and it's going
to be faster.
So that is how references work in a function argument.
Now I want you to go ahead and create a function that takes in an f vector argument, just a regular
f vector.
Then I want you to modify the f vector within the function.
After that, you're going to call the function and pass in an f vector variable.
Then I want you to log the f vector values before and after you make the function call.
Next up, you are going to make that argument into a reference and you're going to take a look at the
difference.
You can do this anywhere you want.
You probably want to do it in the interact function, because that's where we're working right now.
Pause the video, give it a go, and come back when you're done.
All right.
So let's go ahead and create a test function.
It doesn't have to be a member function.
But if you did create one that's also fine.
We'll call this test function.
It's going to take in an f vector.
Let's call it vector.
And inside of here first of all we're going to modify vector.
So we'll say vector dot x is 100.
Vector dot y is 999.
And vector dot z is negative something just like that.
Then we're going to make a call to it.
So here let's get rid of that stuff.
Let's create an f vector here test vector.

And then we're going to, let's see, make a call to test function.
We'll pass in test vector.
And we're going to use these logs as well.
So we'll use the first two before we make the function call.
Actually we don't even need to use the two.
We can just get rid of the extra ones.
And then we'll just print test vector.
Just like that before and after we make the function call.
So the first time we do this obviously because we're using a regular argument, these changes are not
going to affect test vector.

Now we're going to go ahead and change it to a reference compile again.
And now when we play the game you will see that the second time we log, the values are in fact changed
because this time we're using a reference.
So whatever is happening inside of the function is actually impacting the variable as well.
So that is how reference arguments work.
And that is why they're important.
If we take a look at sweep single by channel, you will see that it has this outhit argument and it
is taking in an f hit result.
Essentially what's going to happen is we're going to create this f result struct as a variable.
Then we're going to pass it into sweep single by channel.

And then after the function call we're going to use this variable.

### Else If Statement {#else-if-statement}

```
Hello and welcome.
In this lecture, we're going to learn what an elsif statement is so that we can differentiate the collectible
items from the logs.
Let's go ahead and get started okay.
So inside of this if statement, after we do the shape trace, we're getting a hold of the actor that
was hit by the shape trace.
And now we want to determine if this actor is a collectible item or a lock.
Because what we do is going to be different depending on the type.
If it's an item, we want to pick it up.
If it's a lock actor, we want to try to place down an item that we have.
In order to do this differentiation, we're going to use the tags that we created for these.
If you remember inside of the lock, we created this lock tag.
And inside of the collectible item we created the collectible item tag.
Now we're going to do what we did in Trigger Components.
We're going to use the actor hash tag function in order to determine if the actor has one of these tags,
and based on the tag it has, we're going to determine if it's a collectible item or a lock.
So let's go ahead and check if the hit actor has the collectible item tag.
I'm going to get rid of this log.
And we're going to create an if statement that checks if the hit actor has a tag.
So we're going to call actor hash tag.
And we're going to check for collectible item first.
Just like that.
If this if statement is true, it means that the hit actor is a collectible item.
So we can create a small comment like this.
Next up, we want to check if the hit actor has the lock tag.
What would we do in this case?
Would we create another if statement or would we create an else statement like this?
Well, the else statement is not going to work because this would just mean that the actor doesn't have
the collectible item tag, but it doesn't guarantee that it's going to have the dog tag, so this wouldn't
work.
The next thing we could try is to create another if statement.
We can copy and paste that one and change the tag to be Loc.
This would work, but there is a small optimization problem with this.
When we do the first if check if the hit actor does have the collectible item tag, we don't want to
do this if check.
But with this setup, both of these if checks will be evaluated even if the first one is true.
We don't want that.
We only want to check this if the first one is false.
In order to do something like that, we need to learn how an else if statement works.
So how does an else if statement work?
Here we have a regular if statement.
We know that the condition is going to get evaluated, and if it is true, the body is going to get
executed.
And here is what an else if statement looks like.
It is literally the else keyword.
And then there's an if keyword and then a condition.
When you create an else if it has to be after the if statement.
So you can't just create an else if by itself.
And the way it's going to work is like this.
First of all the condition one is going to get evaluated.
If it is true, the body of the if statement will be done and then the else if will be ignored.
If it is false, then the condition of the else if will be evaluated.
And if it is true, it's going to get executed.
So only if the condition of the if is false, the else if will be evaluated exactly what we need.
And it doesn't stop here.
You can have as many of these else ifs as you want.
You can chain them back to back and these are going to work top to bottom.
Firstly, the if condition will be evaluated.
If it is false, then the else if that is after the if will be evaluated.
If that is false, then the last one will be evaluated in this case.
And at any point, if any of these conditions is true, the body of that condition will be executed
and the rest will be ignored.
So, for example, if the first one is false, then we'll take a look at the second one.
If this is true, then the body of this one will be executed and the third one will be ignored.
And as you add more else ifs, it's going to act in the same way.
And finally, we can create an else statement here.
If all of the conditions are false this is going to be executed.
So this was the final piece of the puzzle when it comes to if statements.
And this is exactly what we need in order to create the logic that we want.
So now I want you to go ahead and turn the two if statements we have into a single else if statement.
And then I want you to log collectible item or log inside of the bodies of those statements.
Pause the video, give it a go, and come back when you're done.
Okay.
The first if statement is fine.
We don't need to change that.
We're simply going to add an else keyword before the second if statement.
Just like that.
Now the first if statement is going to get evaluated.
First.
If it is true the body of that is going to get executed and then the else if will be ignored if the
condition of the if statement is false.
In that case, we're going to take a look at the condition of the else if and if it is true, we're
going to execute the body.
And in this case, the if statement is going to mean that it has the collectible item tag.
And then the else if body is going to mean that the actor has the lock tag.
So we can also create a comment here that says hit actor is a lock actor.
Awesome.
Let's also create the logs.
We can just copy and paste this one and then change it.
Here we'll say collectible item.
And down here we'll say lock actor.
Just like that.

And when we hit a lock actor it is going to print lock actor.
Just like that.
Awesome.
So this means inside of our code here we're able to determine if the actor that we hit with the shape
trace is a collectible item or a lock, which is extremely important because just like I said at the
start of the lecture, we want to do different things depending on the type of actor that we're interacting
with.
That's going to be it.

### Casting {#casting}

```
Hello and welcome.

Collectible item dot h.

And it's going to try to convert to a collectible item pointer.

### TArray {#tarray}

```
Hello and welcome.
In this lecture we're going to learn how to use a t array variable.
And we're going to implement the item pickup functionality.
Let's go ahead and get started.
Okay.
So now that we can determine whether or not we have a collectible item or a lock actor, we need to
go ahead and implement the functionality.
When we click on a collectible item we want to pick it up.
So that is going to be done right here.
And when we click on a lock actor we want to go ahead and place an item down.
If we have the correct key item.
First of all, we need to fix the problem of keeping track of the items that we pick, because we're
going to be able to pick multiple items at the same time.
That means we need to keep track of multiple strings.
In order to do that, we're going to learn how to use a T array.
So far in the course, we only used regular variables that can hold a single value like this f string,
this integer, this float.
These can all hold a single value at any given time.
But what if we want to store multiple values related to each other?
Because that's exactly what we need to do with the inventory system.
We need to store multiple strings that are going to represent the items.
For example, we might have the glass statue.
We might have another statue.
We might have a torch a sword.
So what are we going to do?
Are we just going to create multiple f string variables like this?
No, this isn't going to work because this is going to get very complicated very fast.
First of all, we don't know how many items we're going to have.
So in order to prepare for that we would have to create a ton of empty variables just in case.
And nextly managing these variables is going to be also complicated.
So we're going to have to create a bunch of custom functions as well.
So this is just a bad idea.
What we need to do instead is to use a t array.
This is a variable or a data type that can store multiple values of the same data type in the same variable
in a single variable.
It looks like this.
So you're going to type out t array.
And this is a template type.
As you can see inside of the template argument you're going to provide the data type that you want to
store in this array.
In this case it is an f string.
Then you're going to give the name just like a regular variable.
After you declare it, you can use the add function in order to create a new element inside of the array.
Just like this.
In the parentheses, you're going to provide the element or the string in this case, and you can add
as many items as you want.
Just like that, you don't need to manage multiple variables.
You don't need to have custom functions.
You can just add everything into this single array variable.
And of course we have functions.
To find an item inside of the array, we have a function to remove an item from the array and we're
going to learn those in later lectures.
But in this one we're going to focus on creating the array and adding the collectible item that we clicked
on.

### Section Intro - Battle Blaster {#section-intro-battle-blaster}

```
Hello and welcome to the Battle Blaster.
In this section we're going to build a very fun tank battle game.
First, let's take a look at what the game looks like.
And after that we're going to go through the action plan.
When I start the game we're going to start off with a countdown.
We're going to display this countdown on the screen.
And while that is going on, I'm not going to be able to control the player.
And once it is over, I will be able to move.
In order to create that, we're going to learn how to use UI in unreal.
We're also going to learn how to use timers in C++.
You can see that the player is a tank controlled by the player.
It can rotate, it can aim at different stuff using the mouse, and it can also go forwards and backwards.
We're going to program all of this functionality from scratch.
We're not going to use a pre-made character controller in this section, which is going to be very exciting.
We're also going to implement a shooting system, so we're going to be able to hit the left mouse button
and shoot a projectile.
We're also going to implement a bunch of special effects like the sound effect, the particle effects,
and also a camera shake.
When the projectile hits something we're going to create another character type, which is going to
be the turret that you can see here.
It's also going to be able to shoot, and once we get close enough to it, it's going to rotate and
try to shoot the player.
We're also going to be able to shoot it back, and we can also destroy it, as you can see.
And if the turret manages to hit us a bunch of times, it's also going to be able to destroy the player.
In that case, we're going to print a defeat message on the screen, and we're going to reload the level
that is going to be the lose condition.
And if we can manage to defeat or destroy all of these turrets ourselves within the level, we're going
to display a victory message.
And that's going to be the win condition.
So we're going to keep track of all the turrets in the game, and we're going to keep track of whether
or not we destroyed all of them.
So that is what the game looks like.
Now let's take a look at the action plan.
First of all, we're going to start with a starter project.
We're not going to create something from scratch.
In order to save some time in the starter project, we're going to have a simple level laid out for
us so that we can get right down into the coding part.
We're going to do that by first creating the player tank that is going to be controlled with input.
inputs.
We're going to create all of the custom movement logic that you just saw.
And after that, we're going to move on to the enemy turrets that can detect the player and then rotate
towards the player.
In order to create these we're going to use inheritance.
We're going to create a base type that the player and the enemy is going to inherit from.
And the base type is going to have some functionality that both the player and the enemy is going to
use.
And then we're going to create further logic in the player and enemy classes.
After that, we're going to create the projectile firing system.
We're going to learn how to spawn an actor while the game is running.
And we're also going to learn how to do projectile movement using a special component of unreal.
Next up we're going to create the health, the damage and the destruction system.
We're going to do this by creating a component and using the game framework of unreal.
In order to apply damage to actors.
Then we're going to create the win and lose conditions.
And we're also going to learn how to use the UI system of unreal.
And then we're going to finish things off by creating some special effects.
In this section we're going to learn more about Unreal Engine and the C++ language.
And we're going to take our skills to the next level.
There's a lot of exciting stuff.
Let's go ahead and get started.
```

the game, and then finally there's a sky sphere.

### Base Pawn Class {#base-pawn-class}

```
Hello and welcome.
In this lecture we're going to talk about the inheritance hierarchy that we're going to create for the
tank and the tower.
And then we're going to create the base -- C++ class.
Let's go ahead and get started okay.
So in this game we're going to have two different characters the tank for the player and the tower for
the enemy.
We can go ahead and create two separate C++ classes, one for the tank and one for the tower.
And that would be fine.
But in this game these two have a bunch of common functionality.
For example, they can both fire projectiles, they can both rotate their upper static mesh, and they
both have the same components a capsule for the collision and two static mesh components, one for the
base and one for the turret.
These are the same for both of these characters.
If we create two separate classes, we need to create the same logic, the same code in two different
places, which isn't ideal.
In order to fix this, we're going to use an inheritance based approach.
We're going to create a base pawn C++ class that we're going to use as the parent for the tank and the
tower.
We're going to place all of that shared functionality into this class.
For example the components will be created here.
The root will be the capsule.
And we're going to have two static mesh components, one called base and one called turrets.
We're also going to place the functions that are going to be shared, like the function that is going
to fire projectiles, the function that is going to rotate the turret mesh, and also the function that
is going to play visual effects when the character is destroyed.
All of this shared functionality will be in the base pawn C++ class.
And then we're going to create two different child classes that derive from the base pawn class.
These will be the tank and the tower.
Because of this inheritance, all of that shared functionality that we created in base -- will be
automatically inherited by these classes, so we only need to create it once here instead of creating
in two different places and thus fixing our problem.
On top of this, we're going to create additional functionality in these classes.
For example, the tank is going to have the ability to handle user input.
It's going to have a camera.
It's going to be able to rotate towards the mouse cursor.
And the tower is going to be able to detect the player shoot using a timer instead of player input.
And it's going to be able to rotate towards the player's tank.
All of this specific functionality will be in the child classes, and all of the shared functionality
will be in base pawn.
After we create these, we're going to create blueprints from the tank and the tower, and we're going
to do our customizations in there.
We're going to resize the capsule.
We're going to assign the static mesh components.
And we're going to do more customization.
Because as you know, these types of things are easier to do in blueprints.
So this is the plan.
And the first step is going to be creating the base --.
So let's go ahead and get started with that.
So let's go ahead and create a new class.
We're going to pick a parent class that we can control as a mini challenge.
See if you can guess the type that we're going to use for this.
We're going to use a --.
This is an actor that can be possessed, meaning it can be controlled by the player, which is exactly
what we want to do with this.
You can also see that there's a character class.
This is very similar to the --.
In fact it inherits from the --.
And additionally it has a character movement component, which is great if you want to create a human
like movement, but in this case, it's a little bit of an overkill for us.
We want to create a very simple vehicle movement.
Because of that, we're not going to go with a character and we're going to go with a -- and we're
going to program all of the movement ourselves.
The name of this is going to be base --.
Because this is the first time we're creating code in this project.
It might take a little bit more than usual to get this done.

You can just click on okay.
And then you can click on yes for this.
And then Visual Studio is going to open up.
And here we have our brand new class.

Open up the source file.
And then we're going to take a look at the header.
We have all of the usual functions that we're used to like begin play tick.
There's also a setup player input component which is used to set up the input system inside of the cpp
file.
We have the definitions for all of these functions.
Perfect.
In this lecture we're going to create the components that we're going to use in the base pawn, the
components that are going to be shared for the tank and the tower.
These are going to be a capsule component and two static mesh components, one for the base and one
for the turrets.
First of all, we're going to include the header file for the capsule components.
We need to do this above the generated header right here.
This is important.
This is called components slash capsule components dot h.
And we don't need to include a header file for the static mesh component that is included by default
right here.
Down here we're going to declare a new property visible anywhere.
This is important.
If you don't put this here the component will not be accessible within the details panel in the blueprint.
This will be a new capsule component pointer.
Let's call it capsule components.
And then we're going to create a visible anywhere static mesh component.
Use static mesh component pointer.
This will be the base.
So we'll call it base mesh.
And we're going to copy and paste this and rename it to be turret mesh Just like that.
Next up we're going to create these components within the constructor.
And we're going to set up their attachments and also set the capsule component as the root.
I'm going to give that to you as a challenge.
Go ahead and create the components in the constructor.
And then I want you to set the capsule component as the root component.
After that you're going to attach the base mesh to the capsule and then attach the turret mesh to the
base mesh.
We did this previously.
You should be familiar with the functions that you need to use.
Pause the video, give it a go, and come back when you're done.
All right.
So we're going to use the function createdefaultsubobject in order to create a component.
This is a template function.
So it's going to take in that special argument.
And this argument is going to be the type of the actor the class that we want to create.
First of all we're going to create a new capsule component.
And then we're going to provide the usual parentheses.
And in there we're going to use a text macro to give this a name which is also going to be capsule component.

Also right here and inside of the text macro as well.

And finally, we're not going to attach this to the capsule.
We're going to attach this to the base mesh just like that.
Perfect.

For now, we can just drag this into the level and take a look at its components.

And it also has the base mesh attached to the capsule and the turret mesh attached to the base exactly
as we described.
Perfect.
When we create the child classes, all of this functionality will be carried over, which is exactly
what we want.
We can go ahead and delete this.
We don't need it.

This is going to be good because the spring arm has some built in functionality.
For example, it is going to move the camera automatically if the camera is being obstructed by another
mesh.
So for example, right here, the camera is moving into the character when it is obstructed by this
mesh right here.
So it's not going to go into that mesh and cause a bug.
This is why we need the spring arm on the camera.

### C++ Game Mode {#c-game-mode}

```
Hello and welcome.
In this lecture we're going to create a C++ game mode class and set the BP tank as the default pawn.

At the moment we have this BP tank inside of the level and we're controlling it when we start the game

start actor.
In order to do that though, we need to create a game mode and change the default pawn class.
If you don't remember what the game mode is, let me refresh your memory.
The game mode is an actor that is used to define the rules of the game.
It is created when the level is loaded at the very beginning, and it's going to stay around until you
exit the game or load a different level.
It's a top level manager object that is defining the rules of the game.
For this game, for Battle Blasters, we're going to create a game mode C++ class, and then we're going
to create a blueprint child class from that.
And we're going to set that one as the default game mode.
This time we're going to create a C++ class first, because the game mode is actually going to manage
the rules of the game.
It's going to have its own functionality.
It is going to keep track of how many enemies are left alive in the level.
It's going to display some UI messages on the screen, and it's going to keep track of whether or not
the game is over.
So it's going to have some responsibilities that has to do with the rules of the game.

And we're going to create a new game mode.
If you go into all classes and do a search for game mode, you will see that we have two different options
here the game mode and the game mode base.
For the most part, game mode base is going to be what you want.
Game mode is more specific to multiplayer match based games.
If you're making a game like that, you can go ahead and use the game mode, but for all other games,
you're going to go with the game mode base, which is more bare bones and has room for you to customize
your own functionality.

### Variable Declaration in Condition {#variable-declaration-in-condition}

```
Hello and welcome.
In this lecture, we're going to learn how to declare a variable inside of the condition of an if statement.
Let's go ahead and get started okay.
So in order to understand this we're going to create a test case up here.
So let's create an if statement with an empty condition.
In many cases when we're creating a game we want to create a variable.
Let's say we have a bool here.
And then we want to use it as the condition of an if statement.
Just like this in C++, in order to make this a little bit more concise, we can actually take the declaration
without the semicolon, and then we can place it inside of the condition itself.
Just like that right here.
The variable is going to get declared false, is going to be assigned to it.
And then after that the value of the variable is going to be used as the condition.
In this case my bool is false.
So this if statement is not going to run.
We can actually confirm that by logging something here.
Let's log.
It works.
Just like that, we can compile it, play the game.
We can do a search for it works.
And you can see that it is not printed as we expected.
Now let's go back and set my bool to be true.
Compile again.
This time when we play the game, we should see it works as you can see right here.
So this is how a variable declaration inside of a condition works.
Just like I said, the variable is declared.
It is assigned the value that we provide.
And after that that value is used as the condition.
If it's a true value, the if statement is run.
And we have access to my bool inside of this if statement.
If it is a false value, the if statement is going to get ignored.
This is true for all data types.
So for example, we could have created an integer set it to be zero.
In this case this would be false.
And if we set it something other than zero like ten this would be true.
And it's also the same for pointer types.
So for example I could have created an actor pointer, set it to something valid like get owner.
And this if statement would work and I would have access to the my actor variable in here.
And if I set it to something like null pointer or zero, this pointer would be invalid and the if statement
wouldn't run.
So this is how it works.
And there's an important catch with all of this that has to do with the lifetime of variables inside
of a code block.
What is a code block?
A code block is a pair of curly braces, like the ones we create for if statements and functions.
A local variable that you create inside of a code block is only accessible within that code block.
So for example, if I create an integer here, this integer is going to get deleted at the end of this
code block.
So after this, I'm not going to be able to access a.
As you can see, Visual Studio is giving me a warning here A is undefined because after this code block
A is not accessible anymore.
Because of this rule, the variable that we declare inside of the condition of an if statement is only
going to be accessible inside of the code block that we create for that if statement.
In this case, I'm not going to be able to access my actor outside of the if statement.
As you can see, I am getting an error just like the integer.
So when you are using these types of conditions, you have to be aware that you're not going to be able
to access it outside of it.
Whereas with the previous approach, when we had the declaration outside, we could still access the
variable after the if because in this case we're creating it outside of the if statement, we're creating
it inside of the code block of the function, and in this case we have access to the variable until
the function is over.
So keep that rule in mind.
We can get rid of all of this test code.
And of course the class variables.
The member variables that we declare are not they're not applied to this rule because they're inside
of the class.
They're not declared inside of a local code block like these ones right here.
Okay.
Now we're going to take these declarations that we have here, and we're going to place them into the
if statements.
Let's start with the player controller.
We're going to cut this with control X except the semicolon.
And then we're going to paste it into the if statement just like that.
Now the player controller is declared in this if and it is going to be accessible inside of this code
block right here.
And also the if statement code blocks that are inside of the one that is declared in, I'm going to
do the same thing for the local player and also the subsystem right here.
You're going to do that as a challenge.
Go ahead and place the rest of the declarations into their corresponding if statements.
All right.
We're going to cut this.
Get rid of the semicolon.
Place it into the if and we're going to do it one more time for the subsystem.
We can actually turn this into a single statement.
So instead of declaring the subsystem up here we're going to do it in one go by placing the type here
and getting rid of this one up there.
Now we have the whole thing as a single line.
We're going to take it and place it into the if.
Just like that we can compile it just to see that it works.
But when we play the game it is going to be exactly the same, so no need to test it.
And now you understand how to create a variable declaration inside of an if statement, and the lifetime
of a local variable inside of a code block.
We're going to leave it here for this lecture and continue in the next one.
```

### Forward Declaration {#forward-declaration}

```
Hello and welcome.
In this lecture we're going to learn how to forward declare class types in C++.
Let's go ahead and get started okay.
So here in the tank class we're going to learn how to forward declare a type.
Let's use the camera component as an example.
The type here is you camera components.
In order to forward declare this, we can place the class keyword before the declaration.
Just like that, and with the forward declaration, we're going to be able to use the class type without
including the header file.
That is the point of a forward declaration.
So we now can go ahead and get rid of this.
This code would compile for the header, but now we have to go into the cpp file where we use the camera
component and include the header file here instead.
So in both cases you have to include the header.
But if you have a forward declaration you don't have to place it inside of the header.
You can just place it inside of the cpp file.
You might be asking in that case, what is the purpose of this?
Because it just looks like extra work.
The purpose is performance.
This way forward declaring is supposed to be faster and that is the entire reason this is done.
There's an alternative way of forward declaring.
Let me show you how that works as well.
So instead of placing the class keyword there, we're going to place it up here outside of the class.
And we're going to type out the name of the type which is going to be you camera components just like
that.
And now we can use the type just the same inside of the class.
This is just another way of forward declaring the you camera component type.
And that's it.
That is how to forward declare a class type.
You're going to see this type of code a lot when you're working with unreal, when you're using the
templates or when you're looking up code online.
So it is important that you know how to do this.
And now for your challenge, I want you to go ahead and forward declare the input mapping context variable.
Pause the video, give it a go, and come back when you're done.
All right.
So first of all let's put the class keyword right here in order to forward declare it.
We could also create it up here as we just saw.
We could do this instead of placing it here.
But for the second example, let's use the other way and do it like this.
Now that we forward declare you input mapping context, we can take the header file and place it into
the C++ file.
And that's it.
That is the challenge.
We can go ahead and rebuild this.
Although nothing is going to be different when we play the game.
Great.
So it compiles just as I expected.
And now you understand how to forward declare a class type using the class keyword in two different
places.
You can do it right here or you can do it up here.
Personally, I don't do this very often.
I just include the headers inside of the header just like this.
But like I said, you are going to see this a bunch of times in unreal code.
So it is important that you know what it is so that you're not confused.
In fact, you can see this right here inside of the setup player input component function that we got
from Bass Pawn.
We're going to leave it here for this lecture and continue in the next one.

### Input Action Value Type {#input-action-value-type}

```
Hello and welcome.
In this lecture, we're going to learn how to send values to the C++ functions that we bound to the
input actions okay.
So at the moment when we press the W and S keys we're making a call to the move input function.
Now we want to make the tank move forwards when we press the w key.
And we want to make it move backwards when we press the s key.
There is a small problem with this though.
The problem is inside of this function we don't know which key we're pressing.
Are we pressing w?
Are we pressing S?
In both cases, at the moment we're doing the same thing.
We're logging move inputs.
In order to make this work, we need to send extra information from the input action into this function
right here.
We can do that by using the action value type of an input action.
We're going to open up I a move.
And here we have a property called value type.
By default this is set to be boolean or digital.
But if you click on it you will see that there is other types like float vector two and a vector bool
means that this can only have two states true or false.
When it's not being triggered, it's false, and when it is triggered it is true.
So the only value type we can send to the function with the digital type is going to be true, which
is kind of useless for our case.
Instead of a boolean we're going to use a float.
This float is going to be one when we want to move forward.
So when we press w this value is going to be one.
When we press s when we want to go backwards it is going to be negative one.
We're going to take this value and we're going to multiply it inside of our movement calculation in
order to control the direction of the tank.
So how can we receive this value inside of our C++ code.
Let's take a look at that.
First of all, we need to accept that value as an argument to this function.
We can do that by creating an argument of type f input action value.
This is going to be a reference.
We're going to call it value.
And it's also going to be constant.
The constant keyword means we're not going to be able to change this argument inside of the function's
body.
We're only going to be able to read its value.
Let's copy and paste this into the definition as well.
Now when we trigger the move action, the float value of the move action is going to be sent to our
move input function.
We know that this is going to be a float.
But the argument type here is f input action value.
So let's learn how to extract the float value from this type.
We're going to use the function get.
This is a template function, and the template argument is going to be the data type of the value.
In this case it is a float.
This function call is going to extract the value.
And we're going to save it in a local variable that we're going to call input value just like that.
Let's log this out.
Instead of saying move input as a mini challenge see if you can do that yourself.
So we'll say input value equals percentage f.
And then we're going to provide input value just like that.
Let's go ahead and compile this.
We'll save everything before we exit rebuild.
Now we should be able to play the game.
Let's open up the output log.
And when we hit the W and S keys we're going to print the actual value being sent into that move input
function.
And you can see that value is one.
This is good.
When we press the W key that is the value we want.
But in this case it is also printing one when we press the s key.
In that case we want the value that we sent into the function to be negative one.
In order to control that we need to go into the input mapping context.
And for the S key right here we're going to create a modifier.
So on the modifier line we're going to click on this plus.
And then we're going to pick negate as the modifier.
This modifier is going to multiply the action value with negative one.
So now when we play the game and hit the s key it is going to print negative one instead of one.
And that is exactly what we want.
Now we can use this value inside of our move input function to differentiate between the w and the s
keys.
If the input value is one, we know that the W key is being pressed and we want to go forwards.
And when the input value is negative one, we know that the S key is being pressed and we want to go
backwards.
So we're going to use this input value inside of our movement calculation to control the direction that
way.
Awesome.
So you now understand how to use the value of an input action.
You first need to pick the one that you want inside of the input action asset.
In this case we picked a float.
After that, you're going to modify the mappings that you have.
In this case we wanted to negate this value when we press the S key so that we can differentiate between
these two keys.
And after that inside of your code you need to accept this value as an argument inside of the function
that you bound to that action.
And then you need to use the get function in order to extract the value.
And after that you can use it freely as you want.
We're going to leave it here in this lecture and continue in the next one.
```

### AddActorLocalOffset {#addactorlocaloffset}

```
Hello and welcome back.
In this lecture we're going to make the player tank move forwards and backwards.
Let's go ahead and get started.
Okay.
So now we want to actually make the tank move forwards and backwards.
When we press the W and S keys.
We're of course going to do this inside of the move input function.
We're going to use a function called add actor local offset, which is going to add a location offset
to this actor in the local space.
Before we use this though, I only thought it was necessary to talk about the local space coordinates
so that you understand what they are before we go ahead and use it.
So first of all, inside of unreal we have two types of coordinates the world space coordinates and
the local space coordinates.
The world space is the easier one to understand.
It is the coordinates that we use inside of the level.
So for example, if you click on any of these actors and you click on the root component, you're going
to see a location.
This is the location in the world space.
It is the location used inside of the level.
This location is relative to the center of the level, the 000 location.
All of these actors have a location based on that center.
So for example, this one is at 1200 centimeters to the x -90 on the Y and 70 on the z.
That is based on 000.
Just like I said, the local space coordinates on the other hand, are relative to the component that
another component is attached to.
This is for the components inside of an actor and not the root component.
So for example, in order to understand this, I'm going to bring in a BP tank right here.
We know that this has a root component, the capsule.
Right now the location of this is the location in world space.
But the components inside of the actor like the base mesh, the turret mesh, these are all in local
space.
For example, the base mesh has a location of zero zero and -58.
This is relative to the component that the base mesh is attached to.
In this case the capsule component.
This means that the base mesh is -58cm downwards on the Z, and it doesn't have a different location

### Rotating The Turret {#rotating-the-turret}

```
Hello and welcome back.
In this lecture we're going to make the turret mesh of the player tank rotate towards the mouse cursor.
Let's go ahead and get started.
Okay.
So now that we have this impact point we want to make the turret mesh of the tank rotate towards it.
So how are we going to make this work.
Basically we have two points.
The location of the turret mesh.
Let's say that is called TM.
And then the location of the impact point.
And we're going to call that IP.
What we need to do is we need to get a vector that starts from TM and goes until IP, because that is
going to give us the direction that we need.
That vector is going to look like this.
We're going to get the rotation of this vector.
And we're going to apply that to the turret mesh.
And that is going to essentially rotate the turret mesh towards the impact point.
When you have two points like this and you need a vector that goes from the first one to the second
one.
You can take the second point and you can subtract the first one.
And that is going to give you a vector that looks like this.
So that is some vector math for you.
And like I said, after we get the vector we're simply going to get the rotation and apply it to the
turret mesh.
Let's go ahead and start implementing this.
This is going to be done inside of the base pawn, because both the tank and the enemy is going to rotate
their turret mesh.
The tank is going to rotate towards the impact point of the cursor, and the enemy is going to rotate
towards the tank.
Because of that, this is going to be inside of base pawn.
It's going to be a common functionality.
We're going to create a function called rotate turret.
This is going to take in a vector called look at target.
This vector is going to be the impact point for the tank.
And it is going to be the location of the tank for the enemy.
It is basically the position that we want to rotate towards.
Let's create the definition.
And in here first of all we're going to calculate that vector that goes from the location of the turret
mesh to the look at target.
And like I explained, we can basically take the second position in this case to look at target and
subtract the location of the turret mesh.
We can get the location by calling turret mesh get components location.
This is going to return the location of the component in world space.
Very important that we do this in world space.
This is going to give us the vector we're looking for.
And we're going to save this in a variable that we're going to call two target or vector to target.
Even better.
Next up we're going to get the direction of this vector.
And we can do that by calling a function of the vector class.
And that is called rotation.
This function is going to return us the rotation of this vector as an EF rotator object.
In this case, we want to rotate the mesh around the z axis, and that is going to be the yaw.
So this is essentially the value that we're looking for.
We're going to construct a new EF rotator using that.
And this is going to be the look at rotation.
The final rotation that we're going to apply to the mesh for the pitch and the roll we're going to provide
zero.
And for the yaw we're going to provide this value.
And that is going to be the look at rotation.
Now we can go ahead and apply this to the turret mesh.
So let's call turret mesh.
There's a function called set world rotation.
It's going to accept in an F rotator.
Just like that.
You can see that the name of this function is called set world rotation.
That is why it was important for us to do this calculation in world space, because this function is
going to set the rotation in world space.
Now we have this function called rotate turret that is going to accept a position as a vector.
And it's going to rotate the turret mesh towards that vector.
The next step is going to be calling this from the tank so that we actually use it.
And that is going to be your challenge.
Go ahead and call the rotate turret function to make the tank turret rotate towards the impact point
that we calculated.
Pause the video, give it a go, and come back when you're done.
All right we're going to go into the tank dot cpp file.
And here in tick we're going to make a call to rotate turret.
We can do this because the tank is inheriting from base pawn.
And because of that of course we have access to all of the functions of the base pawn inside of the
tank as well.
We're going to do this after we call get hit result under cursor.
And we're going to call rotate turret with the hit results impact point.
Just like that.
And we no longer need to draw the debug sphere with this.
Every frame of the game, the tank is going to calculate the hit location of the mouse cursor, and

Let's go ahead and exit out of unreal and rebuild the project.
Now we should be able to play the game, and you will see that the turret mesh is being rotated towards
the mouse cursor.
Perfect.
At the moment.
If we click into the viewport, the mouse cursor is going to disappear, which makes it a little bit
more difficult to control it.
But don't worry, we're going to fix that.
The main issue I have at the moment is if you start using the mouse too fast, you're going to see that
the turret mesh is snapping and it looks very ugly.
So let's go ahead and fix that as well.
Inside of the rotate turret function.
Instead of setting the rotation immediately, we're going to interpolate it so that we change rotation
in a smoother way.
In order to do this, we can use a function from the F math class.
This function is called r Interp two.
The R stands for rotator.
So this function is going to take in an F rotator, and it's going to interpolate it towards a target
one.
The first argument is the current rotation that we are in.
The second one is the target rotation that we want to interpolate towards.
And then we're going to provide the delta time.
And then the speed that we want to interpolate at the current rotation.
We can get it by calling turret meshes get component rotation function.
The target rotator is going to be the look at rotation.
Of course after that we're going to provide delta time.
Let's actually put all of these arguments on their own lines.
To get the delta time.
We can call get world Get delta seconds just like that.
And finally, the interpolation speed can be something like ten.
If that is too slow or too fast, we can change it later.
This function is going to return a new F rotator, the interpolated one, and we're going to save that
in a new variable that we're going to call interpolated rotation just like that.
And we're going to use this one instead of the look at rotation.
With this, instead of setting the rotation to the lookat rotation immediately, we're slowly going
to interpolate towards it and it is going to look much nicer.
Let's compile it.
Play the game.
And now you will see that the turret is interpolating towards the cursor, and it's looking much nicer.
And even if I start moving the mouse really fast, it doesn't really snap, which is what we wanted
to achieve.
