### While Loop {#while-loop}

```
Hello and welcome.
In this lecture we're going to learn how to write a while loop in C++.
Let's go ahead and get started okay, so now that we have all of the enemy actors inside of this towers
array, we want to go through all of them one by one and set their tank variable to the one that we
have right here.
In order to do this effectively, we're going to learn the concept of a loop.
In programming in C++ we have different types of loops, mainly the while loop and the for loop.
In this lecture we're going to focus on the while and later on we're going to learn the for loop as
well.
A loop is going to let us execute the same piece of code over and over again, using a condition.
In order to create a while, we're going to type the while keyword.
We're going to put a set of parentheses, and then we're going to create a code block just like this.
The parentheses is going to have the condition.
This is similar to the condition of an if statement.
If the expression inside of here is true, the loop is going to continue looping.
If it is false, the loop is going to exit.
Once the loop exits, the code execution is going to continue after the body of the loop.
In order to visualize the start and the end of the loop, let's go ahead and log something out at the
start and at the end as well.
We'll say start of the loop and down here we'll say end of the loop.
Now let me explain to you how the loop is going to work.
First of all, let's give something like true for the condition and let's put something inside of the
body as well.
For now, we can just place a log.
Actually, let's also place some semicolons there.
And we'll say body of the loop.
Body of the loop just like that.
When the code execution reaches this line, the condition is going to be evaluated.
If it is true, the body of the loop will be executed.
If it is false, execution is going to continue after the body.
Let's say that it is true the body will be executed in this case, and this log statement will be called.
Once we reach the end of the body, we're going to go back to the condition and re-evaluate.
Is it still true?
If it is, we're going to do the body again and then we're going to go back to the condition one more
time, reevaluate.
Is it still true?
Yes.
And then if it is true, like I said, do the body again reevaluate.
Is it true?
Yes.
Do the body again until we hit a false in the condition.
In that case, we're going to exit.
What we have right here is actually called an infinite loop, because the condition is always going
to be true.
Do not compile this code.
If you compile it and run it, your program is going to be stuck inside of this loop and you're not
going to be able to exit it, which isn't going to be fun, trust me.
Instead of having true right here and creating an infinite loop, we usually have a condition that makes
sense.
When we create loops, we usually create index variables as well, and these are usually integers.
Let's call this loop index.
We're going to initialize this to zero at the start and at the end of the loop body.
We're going to increase this by one just like that.
Now in each iteration of the loop the index is going to go up by one.
So it's going to start at zero.
It's going to be 12345 and continue getting incremented like that.
Using this variable we can write a condition that starts off true.
But as the loop index goes up, becomes false, and writing that condition is going to be your challenge.
Go ahead and write the condition that checks if loop index is less than ten.
Pause the video, give it a go, and come back when you're done.
All right, let's get rid of this.
True.
And we're going to type loop index less than ten just like that.
And now we actually have a loop that is going to be able to exit and not create an infinite loop which
is great.
Let me explain how this is going to work.
So as we execute the code, we're going to come from up here down to the loop.
We're going to print start of the loop.
And then we're going to create loop index.
And after that we're going to get into the loop.
In the first go we're going to check the condition is loop index less than ten.
In this case yes.
Because loop index is zero.
We're going to go into the body of the loop.
We're going to log this message.
And then we're going to increase loop index by one.
So now loop index goes up to one.
At the end of the loop we're going to go back to the condition.
And we're going to check again is loop index less than ten.
Yes because one is less than ten.
In that case, we're going to go through the body again.
This is going to cause the loop index to go up to two.
Then we will go back to the condition is to less than ten.
Yes it is.
Go through the body again.
Now it's three.
And we're going to keep doing this until the loop index becomes ten.
In that case, when we go back and check the condition is ten less than ten.
Well no it's not.
It's equal to ten.
In that case the loop is going to exit.
And we're going to continue executing after the loop body.
And we're going to print end of the loop.
So that is how a while loop works.
Let's actually log the loop index instead of logging body of the loop.
So I will change this to loop index percentage d for an integer.
We'll give it the loop index just like that.
Let's compile this.
Let's go ahead and play the game.
If you take a look at the output log, you will see that we logged zero all the way to nine.
And at the end of nine we printed end of the loop because the loop was over.
And of course at the start we printed start of the loop just as we expected.
So using a loop, you can see how it makes it much easier to run a certain piece of code over and over
again using a condition.
Like I said, loops can be a little dangerous if you create an infinite loop and it can be a little
bit difficult to debug that as well.
So be careful with the condition.
Now all there is left to do is using this loop knowledge in order to loop over the towers array.
And we're going to learn how to do that in an upcoming lecture.
For this one we're going to leave it here.
```

### Rotating Towards Player {#rotating-towards-player}

```
Hello and welcome.
In this lecture, we're going to make the enemy towers rotate towards the player when the player is
close enough.
Let's go ahead and get started okay.
So in order to make the enemy towers turn, we essentially did all of the difficult work beforehand.
Inside of the base spawn, we created the rotate function, and inside of the game mode, we looped
over all of the tower actors and we gave them the tank pointer.
Now all we have to do is bring all of this together inside of the towers tick function.
We're going to do this in tick, because we want the enemy towers to rotate towards the player continuously
all the time.
Because the player is not going to be in one place, it is going to be moving.
In order to make this happen, we can actually go ahead and make a call to the rotate turret function.
And the location that we need to pass is the location of the tank, which we can get by calling Get
Actor location on the tank pointer that we got from the game mode.
Let's save this and compile it.
Let's play the game and you will see that the enemy towers are going to rotate immediately to the player
tank.
But there is a small problem with this.
They are going to rotate towards us even if we are very far away like here.
We don't really want this to happen, we only want them to rotate towards us if we are close enough,
and we want to control that distance using a variable as well.
So how are we going to implement this?
First of all, we're going to take a look at the location of the tower.
And then we're going to take a look at the location of the tank.
We're going to calculate this distance.
This is all going to happen inside of the tick function.
Let's call that the distance to player DP.
If the distance to player is less than the fire range of the enemy tower, that is going to be a variable.
We're going to create fire range.
If this is true, we're going to rotate towards the player.
If it is not true, we're not going to do anything.
So that is how this is going to work.
Let's go into the enemy tower class and start implementing this.
First of all I'm going to create that fire range variable.
This is going to be a uproperty edit anywhere so that we can change it in the editor.
It is going to be a float called Fire Range.
And by default we can set this to be something like 300 for three meters of range, which is going to
mean the enemy is going to rotate to the player only if the player is closer than three meters.
Next up, inside of the tick function, we are going to calculate the distance from the player to the
tower or from the tower to the player.
It's going to be the same thing.
One thing I didn't do here, I did not check if the tank was valid.
It should be valid, but you never know.
So let's add that check right there.
```

```
the game, all of the towers start their timers and start shooting at the player.
```

### Hit Events {#hit-events}


### OnTakeAnyDamage {#ontakeanydamage}

```
Hello and welcome.
In this lecture we're going to learn how to use the on take any damage delegate.
And we're going to destroy actors when they lose all of their health.
Let's go ahead and get started okay.
So now that we're making a call to the apply damage function and we are generating the damage event,
let's go ahead and learn how to use the on take any damage delegate.
First of all this delegate is going to be on the tower actor if that is the one that is getting damaged
or it is going to be on the tank actor if the tower is shooting the tank.
In this case, let's say that the tower is getting damaged.
It has the on take any damage delegate, and it also has the health component.
On the health component, we're going to create a function called on damage taken.
This function is going to be bound to the on take any damage delegate.
```

### Show Mouse Cursor {#show-mouse-cursor}

```

from the player controller.

In this case, the pawn is the tank.
```

```
The first thing that you need to write is the condition.
In this case, it is going to be is victory.
After the condition, you're going to put a question mark, and then you're going to put the value of
the true condition, which is going to be victory.
After this you're going to put a colon and you're going to type out the value of the false condition,
which is going to be defeat.
At the end we're going to put a semicolon.
And this is how a ternary operator looks or a ternary statement looks.
First you have the condition.
Then you have the value that you want.
If the condition is true then you have the value that you want if the condition is false.
Just like I said, it is very much like an if else statement.
This is the condition.
The whole expression is going to return victory.
If is victory is true, it is going to return defeat.
If is victory is false.
So this would be the else case.
And this would be the if case and this would be the condition.
The cool thing about the ternary is that it is very concise.
As you can see, it is only a single statement instead of A12345678 line statement.
Obviously the if else is much more powerful because you can actually have a code block.
In the case of a ternary, you can have a single expression here.
Great.
```

### Countdown Timer {#countdown-timer}

```
Hello and welcome back.
In this lecture we're going to create a countdown timer at the start of the level.
So when I play the game you're going to see the countdown going off.
And once it reaches zero it says go.
And the game begins.

First of all, when we start a level we're not going to be able to control the player.
So we're going to turn off the player's input at the very start.
At the same time, we're going to start a timer that is going to count down from a number that we pick.
Let's say that number is three seconds.
This timer is going to time out every second and it's going to reduce the countdown seconds.
So the first time it times out it's going to go down to two, then to one, then to zero.
And when it reaches zero we're going to enable the player and start the game.
We're going to display these numbers on the screen in the end, but for now, we're just going to log
them in the output log.
The first step is going to be disabling the player at the start of the game.

Begin play.

enabled we're using the player controller.
So if we try to call it before we get it, it's not going to work.
So this is the first step.
And then we're going to create the timer inside of the game mode here at the end of Beginplay.
For this purpose we're first going to create a bunch of variables and a function.

We're going to wait.

This is not going to have a default value.

We're going to set this from the editor.

of Beginplay.
Before we set the timer, we're going to set the countdown seconds to countdown delay.

this timer.

If we create the timer handle here as a local variable, we're not going to be able to use it in a different
function.
```

### Playing Sounds {#playing-sounds}

```
Hello and welcome.
In this lecture we're going to learn how to play sound effects.
Let's go ahead and get started okay.
So inside of the audio folder in the assets folder we have a couple of sound effects.
We're going to play the explode audio.
When the tank or the tower gets destroyed.
And we're going to play the thawed audio when we launch a projectile.
And also when the projectile hits something.
We're going to do this inside of our code.
So let's go into Visual Studio.
First of all, we will implement the sounds for the projectile.
The first step is going to be creating a couple of edit anywhere variables, so that we can assign those
assets from the editor.
We'll do this after the hit particles.
The type is going to be a u sound base pointer.
We don't need to include any headers for this.
This is going to be launch sound.
And then we're going to create one more called the hit sound.
In this example we're going to use the same sound effect for the launch sound and the hit sound, but
in reality, you probably want to play two different sounds for the launching and the hitting of the
projectile.
And because of that, we're going to create two separate variables.
That's all we need to do here.
We can save this and go into the cpp file.
Here we need to have the gameplay statics.

When we first create the projectile that's going to be the launch sound.

statics.
```
